Meanwhile , it 's pushing ever farther into the Arctic Ocean in search of more oil and gas . <p> " We know there is a paradox , " admits Vidar Helgesen , Norway 's climate and energy minister . " We have been living well from oil and gas . But there is no country in the world that has done more to undermine the oil and gas industry than Norway . " <p> The mountainous Scandinavian country of 5 million people is torn between its ambition to be a global leader on climate change and the awareness that its wealth is linked to the world 's dependence on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hub of an offshore industry that has made Norway the world 's eighth biggest exporter of oil and third biggest exporter of natural gas . Norway 's $875 billion oil kitty is the world 's largest sovereign wealth fund , and hydrocarbons account for 40 percent of Norwegian exports . <p> But very little of those fossil fuels are used at home . Like the rest of the country , Stavanger gets almost all its electricity from hydropower . <p> And the streams of Teslas driven by oil workers through the streets of Stavanger attest to the rich subsidies the government has poured into the electric car market . E-cars have zero import duty , sales tax is a quarter less than for conventional vehicles and most roads are free . <p> Some 29 percent of new cars sold in Norway are electric or hybrid . The government in June introduced a target of 100 percent by 2025 . <p> Helgesen says Norway 's example is spreading around the world , with once-skeptical car manufacturers investing in green technology and speeding the transition away from hydrocarbons . <h> Related Content <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to become carbon neutral by 2030 " some 20 years ahead of schedule . <p> Norway is also one of the most generous donors to international initiatives to maintain rainforests , which help fight climate change by absorbing some of the heat-trapping carbon dioxide released by the burning of fossil fuels . <p> It has already spent $1 billion saving trees in Brazil and is committed to spend up to $350 million a year preserving trees in places like Indonesia and Guyana . <p> In fact , Norway was one of the few Western countries to see a rise in domestic carbon emissions in 2015 . That was mainly due to the fact that its aging North Sea oil fields require ever more energy to tap depleting reservoirs . <p> " The plan has always been to buy carbon credits to allow us to continue polluting as a country , " says Lars Haltbrekken , chairman of the Norwegian chapter of Friends of the Earth , an environmental advocacy group . <p> " That is why we do n't think carbon neutrality is the most important factor in combatting climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in developing countries . It does n't require us to reduce emissions here in Norway . " <p> A tougher European Union scheme will set emissions reduction targets within the next two years . But Norway , which is not an EU member , will still be able to trade emissions credits with European neighbors to reach its reduction quota . <p> Meanwhile , environmental activists bristle at exploration permits handed out to 13 oil companies in May to drill in a new area of the Norwegian Arctic . Critics say the technology to safely explore in such remote areas is not properly tested and claim plunging prices make Arctic oil unaffordable without hefty Norwegian subsidies . While companies pay 78 percent tax on hydrocarbons they produce in Norwegian waters , they can claim back the same amount on costs for exploration . <p> " We absolutely regard this as a subsidy , " says Ellen Viseth , a political advisor at the Bellona environmental group . <p> Viseth says the Barents Sea north of Norway is already one of the most expensive places in the world to produce oil . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , " she says . " But the Norwegian government is heavily supporting the oil and gas industry . The oil companies do n't take much of a risk but their upside is huge . " <p> Norway 's state-owned energy company Statoil scooped up the largest share of exploration rights in the latest licensing round . Chevron and ConoccoPhillips also secured permits . <p> In June , Norway became one of the first countries to ratify last year 's Paris Agreement on climate change , which seeks to limit global warming to less than 2 degrees C ( 3.6 degrees F ) compared with pre-industrial times . <p> Haltbrekken says that any Norwegian contribution to this target that does not involve a reduction in oil and gas is empty . <p> Helgesen disagrees . <p> " We are living in a time of tremendous energy transformation , " he says . " We want to play a part whether it is in electrification , bio-energy , hydropower , or any other green energy . But Norway has the cleanest hydrocarbons anywhere in the world . As well as battling the rising sea , one of the biggest challenges is a lack of fresh water and electricity . <p> " The solar facility we 've installed in Majuro is designed to do two things - firstly to harness solar energy panels , and secondly the way that we 've designed it is to collect rainwater , which enables the rainwater to be fed directly into the reservoirs over which the solar panels sit , " says Sunergise chief executive Paul Makumbe . <p> The reservoirs in Majuro are bordered by solar panels , which have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ down into the reservoirs . <p> " I think if you look at it this way it will save them probably about 636,000 litres of diesel per year , which directly goes to benefit the country because they 're not importing that diesel , " says Mr Makumbe . <p> " And then that 'll save about 650 to 700 tonnes of carbon . So if you think about climate change issues , you 're doing both - you 're helping from an economic side but also from a climate change perspective . " <p> The concept is the brainchild of Kiwi company Sunergise , which also has systems in place in Nauru and Fiji and says it could be replicated in communities that rely on tank water all over the Pacific and in New Zealand . <p> The use of solar energy is not unique , but the way it has been combined to collect freshwater is the first of its kind - and Mr Makumbe says innovation like that is vital . <p> " It helps them save in diesel , save their costs - costs which they can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ there will be a direct benefit to the people of the Marshall Islands . " <p> Majuro has declared a state of disaster several times because of fresh water shortages , but this is one step to stop the islands from running dry . 
@@13637724 <h> Last woolly mammoths wiped out by lack of water <p> One of the last known groups of woolly mammoths died out because of a lack of drinking water , scientists believe . <p> Mammoths on St Paul Island , in the Bering Sea , lasted 5000 years longer than most species . Photo : Wikimedia commons <p> The Ice Age beasts were living on a remote island off the coast of Alaska , and scientists have dated their demise to about 5600 years ago . <p> They believe that a warming climate caused lakes to become shallower , leaving the animals unable to quench their thirst . <p> Most of world 's woolly mammoths had died out by about 10,500 years ago . <p> Scientists believe that human hunting and environmental changes played a role in their extinction . <p> But the group living on St Paul Island , which is located in the Bering Sea , managed to cling on for another 5000 years . <p> This study in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences suggests that these animals faced a different threat from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ after the Ice Age , sea levels rose , causing the mammoths ' island home to shrink in size . <p> This meant that some lakes were lost to the ocean , and as salt water flowed into the remaining reservoirs , freshwater diminished further . <p> The fur-covered giants were forced to share the ever-scarcer watering holes . But their over-use also caused a major problem . <h> Congregating mammoths <p> Lead author Prof Russell Graham , from Pennsylvania State University , said : " As the other lakes dried up , the animals congregated around the water holes . <p> " They were milling around , which would destroy the vegetation - we see this with modern elephants . <p> " And this allows for the erosion of sediments to go into the lake , which is creating less and less fresh water , making the lake less shallow . <p> " The mammoths were contributing to their own demise . " <p> He said that if there was not enough rain or melting snow to top the lakes up , the animals may have died very quickly . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and 200 litres of water daily , " Prof Graham said . <p> " We assume mammoths did the same thing . It would n't have taken long if the water hole had dried up . If it had only dried up for a month , it could have been fatal . " <p> The researchers say climate change happening today could have a similar impact on small islands , with a threat to freshwater putting both animals and humans at risk . <h> ' Best understood extinction ' <p> Commenting on the study , Love Dalen , professor in evolutionary genetics at the Swedish Museum of Natural History , said : " With this paper , the St Paul Island mammoth population likely represents the most well-described and best understood prehistoric extinction events . <p> " In a broader perspective , this study highlights that small populations are very sensitive to changes in the environment . " <p> The very last surviving mammoths lived on Wrangel Island , in the Arctic Ocean . It is thought they died out 4,000 years ago . <h> Related Stories <p> Professor Adrian Lister @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world 's foremost experts on mammoths . He worked on the first scientific study of mammoth DNA , but believes there are too many ... Audio

" <p> South Australia 's agricultural sector will be impacted by warming and drying conditions , increased risks around drought and bushfire , as well as changing pest and disease risks . <p> Regional adaptation plans consider the risks to specific industries in each part of the state . Agricultural industries are already working together with DEWNR , Natural Resource Management Boards and Primary Industries and Regional SA ( PIRSA ) to plan for and address these risks , through research and development into increased water use efficiency , incorporating updated or new farming practices , looking at alternate crop types and improving business management and diversification to manage risk . <p> Horse SA is a community based non-profit organisation working with and for horse owners on a range of topics and issues of common interest , including climate change . " <p> He 's just received more than $110,000 for more than 6000 carbon units grown in forest blocks he planted in South Taranaki . @@13649966 <h> International report confirms 2015 warmest year on record <p> A new State of the Climate report confirmed that 2015 surpassed 2014 as the warmest year since at least the mid-to-late 19th century . <p> Last year 's record heat resulted from the combined influence of long-term global warming and one of the strongest El Ni ? o events the globe has experienced since at least 1950 . The report found that most indicators of climate change continued to reflect trends consistent with a warming planet . Several markers such as land and ocean temperatures , sea levels and greenhouse gases broke records set just one year prior . <p> These key findings and others are available from the State of the Climate in 2015 report released online today by the American Meteorological Society ( AMS ) . <p> The report , led by NOAA National Centers for Environmental Information , is based on contributions from more than 450 scientists from 62 countries around the world and reflects tens of thousands of measurements from multiple independent datasets ( highlights , full report ( link is external ) ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , notable weather events and other data collected by environmental monitoring stations and instruments located on land , water , ice and in space . <p> " This ' annual physical ' of Earth 's climate system showed us that 2015 's climate was shaped both by long-term change and an El Ni ? o event , " said Thomas R. Karl , L.H.D. , Director , NOAA National Centers for Environmental Information . " When we think about being climate resilient , both of these time scales are important to consider . Last year 's El Ni ? o was a clear reminder of how short-term events can amplify the relative influence and impacts stemming from longer-term global warming trends . " <p> The report 's climate indicators show patterns , changes , and trends of the global climate system . Examples of the indicators include various types of greenhouse gases ; temperatures throughout the atmosphere , ocean , and land ; cloud cover ; sea level ; ocean salinity ; sea ice extent ; and snow cover . <p> Report highlights include these indications of a warming planet : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ gas concentrations , including carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) , methane and nitrous oxide , rose to new record high values during 2015 . The annual average atmospheric CO2 concentration at Mauna Loa , Hawaii , the location of the world 's longest direct measurements of CO2 , was 400.8 parts per million ( ppm ) , which surpassed 400 ppm for the first time . This was 3.1 ppm more than 2014 , and was the largest annual increase observed in the 58-year record . The 2015 average global CO2 concentration was not far below , at 399.4 ppm , an increase of 2.2 ppm compared with 2014 . <p> * Global surface temperature highest on record . Aided by the strong El Ni ? o , the 2015 annual global surface temperature hit record warmth for the second consecutive year , easily surpassing the previous record set in 2014 by more than 0.1 ? C ( 0.2 ? F ) . This exceeded the average for the mid- to late 19th century -- commonly considered representative of preindustrial conditions -- by more than 1 ? C ( 1.8 ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , record to near-record warmth was reported across every inhabited continent . <p> * Sea surface temperatures highest on record . The globally averaged sea surface temperature was also the highest on record , breaking the previous mark set in 2014 . The highest temperature departures from average occurred in part of the northeast Pacific , continuing anomalous warmth there since 2013 , and in part of the eastern equatorial Pacific , reflective of the dominant El Ni ? o . The North Atlantic southeast of Greenland remained colder than average and was colder than 2014 . <p> * Global upper ocean heat content highest on record . Globally , upper ocean heat content exceeded the record set in 2014 , reflecting the continuing accumulation of thermal energy in the upper layer of the oceans . Oceans absorb over 90 percent of Earth 's excess heat from global warming . <p> * Global sea level highest on record . Global average sea level rose to a new record high in 2015 and was about 70 mm ( about 2 ? inches ) higher than the 1993 average , the year that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the past two decades , sea level has increased at an average rate of 3.3 mm ( about 0.15 inch ) per year , with the highest rates of increase in the western Pacific and Indian Oceans . <p> * Extremes were observed in the water cycle and precipitation . A general increase in the water cycle , combined with the strong El Ni ? o , enhanced precipitation variability around the world . An above-normal rainy season led to major floods in many parts of the world . But globally , areas in " severe " drought rose from 8 percent in 2014 to 14 percent in 2015 . <p> The report also documents key regional climate and climate-related events : <p> * The Arctic continued to warm ; sea ice extent remained low . The Arctic land surface temperature tied with 2007 and 2011 as the highest since records began in the early 20th century , representing a 2.8 ? C ( 5 ? F ) increase since that time . Average sea surface temperatures across the Arctic Ocean during August in ice-free regions ranged from near normal to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to decreasing Arctic sea ice extent and thickness . In February 2015 , the maximum sea ice extent reached its largest footprint of the year , and was the smallest in the 37-year satellite record , at 7 percent below the 1981 -- 2010 average . Minimum sea ice extent in September 2015 was 29 percent less than the 1981 -- 2010 average and the fourth lowest value on record . <p> * Arctic animals and fish were impacted by changing climate . As a consequence of sea ice retreat from warming oceans , vast walrus herds in the Pacific Arctic are hauling out on land rather than on sea ice , raising concern about the energy requirements of females and young animals . Increasing temperatures in the Barents Sea are linked to a shift in fish populations : warmer-water fish are moving farther north , and long-standing Arctic species , such as gelatinous snailfish and polar cod have been almost pushed out of the area . <p> * Widespread harmful algal bloom impacts northeast Pacific . In the late spring and summer 2015 , a widespread harmful algal bloom ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ America from central California to British Columbia , Canada , resulted in significant impacts to marine life , coastal resources and the human communities that depend on these resources . Unusually warm surface water in the Pacific is considered a factor regarding the severity and early onset of the bloom . <p> * Antarctic temperatures were colder than average ; sea ice extent was highly variable . Temperatures across Antarctica were lower than the 1981 -- 2010 average for most of the year . Antarctic sea ice extent and area had large variability during the year , shifting from record high levels in May to record low levels in August . <p> * Global ice and snow cover decline . Preliminary data indicate that 2015 was the 36th consecutive year of overall alpine glacier retreat across the globe . Across the Northern Hemisphere , late-spring snow cover extent continued its trend of decline , with June the second lowest in the 49-year satellite record . Below the surface , record high temperatures at the 20-meter ( 65-feet ) depth were measured at all permafrost observatories on the North Slope of Alaska @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( 1.2 ? F ) per decade since 2000 . <p> * Tropical cyclones were well above average overall . There were 101 tropical cyclones across all ocean basins in 2015 , well above the 1981 -- 2010 average of 82 storms . The eastern/central Pacific had 26 named storms , the most since 1992 . The western North Pacific and north and south Indian Ocean basins also saw high activity . Similar to 2013 and 2014 , the North Atlantic season was quieter than most years of the last two decades with respect to the number of storms . The overall strength of the North Atlantic storms were weaker as well , just 68 percent of the 1981 -- 2010 median seasonal value , with Major Hurricane Joaquin accounting for nearly one-half that value . <p> The State of the Climate in 2015 is the 26th edition in a peer-reviewed series published annually as a special supplement to the Bulletin of the American Meteorological Society . @@13657811 <p> Above all , David Robie 's Do n't Spoil My Beautiful Face : Media , Mayhem and Human Rights in the Pacific is a damning indictment of the parlous state of affairs in parts of this region . <p> The book is also a telling account of the continuous failure of leadership on a fairly grand scale , with ordinary people bearing the brunt of it . <p> Dr Robie , professor of journalism at the Auckland University of Technology and director of the Pacific Media Centre , deals with the vital issues of environmental degradation , media censorship , social chaos and human suffering ( largely caused by bad governance ) , various types of violent and nonviolent conflicts , and colonialism and neocolonialism . <p> Allegedly apathetic international and local media also attract some flak . Robie , who has a long record of service in the Pacific Islands , laments that a region with so much promise due to its relative tranquility , natural beauty , and richness of culture has been in such a prolonged state of decline , despite the postindependence optimisms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ civil uprisings , and corruption ; the region 's pristine environments damaged by nuclear testing , wanton resource exploitation , and the spectre of climate change ; and indigenous cultures threatened by the twin forces of neocolonialism and neoliberal economics . <p> These adversities are superimposed on growing incidences of human rights abuses and draconian media legislation in some countries . <p> The looming threats of global warming and sea-level rise only complicate matters . <p> Robie has been reporting these trends in the Asia-Pacific region since the 1980s , both as a journalist and as a media educator , covering self-determination for indigenous minorities in New Caledonia , the struggles in Timor-Leste and West Papua , the Bougainville rebellion , nuclear testing in French Polynesia and the Marshall Islands , and the ethnically motivated coups in Fiji . <p> Documented conflicts Some of these conflicts are documented in his earlier books : Blood on the Banner ( 1980 ) highlighted indigenous Pacific Islanders ' struggle against the remnants of colonialism , while TuGalala : Social Change in the Pacific ( 1992 ) depicted a continuing battle against environmental catastrophe , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and neocolonialism . <p> In Do n't Spoil My Beautiful Face , Robie reproduces some of his previous writings as a yardstick and a backdrop for deeper insights into the Pacific 's seemingly intractable problems . Reflecting on his two-and-a-half decades of Asia-Pacific coverage , Robie intones , " Sadly , not a lot has changed " ( page 6 ) . <p> He adds , " Political independence has not necessarily rid the Pacific of the problems that it faces , and in many cases , Pacific political leaders are themselves part of the problem " ( 27 ) . One of the more startling statistics , at least for the uninitiated , is the deaths of an estimated 120,000 Pacific Islanders in various disputes over the past quarter-century , plus another 200,000 when Timor-Leste is included ( 311 ) . <p> If the narrative sounds depressing , it is regrettably all too predictable : long-term ethnic and political tensions coupled with low growth rates and underdevelopment are usually fodder for violent conflict in fragile states ( see Securing a Peaceful Pacific , by John Henderson and Greg Watson @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ blame for these serious issues squarely on various corrupt Pacific Island leaders , whom he views as having been part of the problem for far too long . But it is not only rogue Pacific Island leaders who are causing problems . <p> Robie also faults leaders from developed countries for their inaction in the face of what he describes as a litany of tortures , murders , exploitation , rapes , military raids , and arbitrary arrests . Most affected is West Papua , where the brutal repression of the native Melanesians by the Indonesian security services is well documented . <p> The book reminds us why the Pacific is still struggling despite copious amounts of bilateral aid over the decades . It is in the interest of nuclear powers France and the United States to keep their territories in a dependent state in order to further their own military , economic , and geopolitical ambitions . <p> Exploitation ' normalised ' ? The question is whether the exploitation of Island countries by economically and militarily powerful nations has become normalised . Recently , Australia and New Zealand remained unmoved @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ countries ' apparent intransigence regarding a joint proposal by Pacific Island nations for a tougher global target on greenhouse gas emissions . Is the world resigned to the bullying and maltreatment of Pacific Island nations by the bigger powers ? <p> Robie does not hide his disappointment with what he sees as the media 's failure to tackle these crucial issues . <p> He feels that the international media ignore or underreport major issues , such as Indonesian repression of West Papua and the assassination of journalists in the Philippines . In his eyes , Australian media failed to sufficiently probe their country 's 2006 security treaty with Indonesia . Robie insists that the treaty led to Australia 's overt repression of pro-independence Papuan activists . <p> To be fair to the media , the Indonesian government has banned foreign journalists from West Papua for years . However , Robie argues that the problem goes deeper . He links it to the media 's commercially driven priorities , which he feels supersede social , humanitarian , and public-interest obligations . <p> Under this journalistic framework , West Papua would be deemed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ politically . <p> Do n't Spoil My Beautiful Face tries to prod the media 's conscience by highlighting the suffering in the region . Robie advocates a new , more thorough , considered , and inclusive reporting approach , which he describes as " critical development journalism . " <p> This proposed framework sources grassroots rather than just elite views , emphasises conflict resolution , promotes human rights , and supports development ( 325 -- 330 ) . <p> Empowered media Robie 's views are consistent with a growing recognition among some policy makers that an empowered media could play an important role in regional development , especially in politically fragile Island societies . However , new ideas often face resistance . <p> As a result of challenging the orthodoxy , Robie has attracted criticism from some traditionalists , who believe that concepts such as " peace journalism " contravene media objectivity , what with Bainimarama 's military-backed government in Fiji touting its version of " journalism of hope , " fueling suspicions about government control of the media under the guises of stability and development . <p> Robie is adamant @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ neither would it pander to political slogans such as " cultural sensitivity , " which he sees as a cover-up for abuse of power . <p> To the contrary , Robie envisions an approach based on a greater level of intensive journalism focused on exposing the truth , reporting on alternatives , and offering solutions . Robie 's central thesis is that the Pacific is caught in a vicious cycle of conflicts and underdevelopment . <p> Traditional subsistence lifestyles have been under sustained pressure from globalisation and other forces . The media are duty-bound to keep track of these trends and draw attention to them , but their response has largely been inadequate . <p> Robie is calling for a new media strategy , based on greater journalistic effort , commitment , and foresight . Some may question whether such a new direction is even possible , given media 's entrenched , deadline-driven , profit-focused economic model . <p> These ideological arguments aside , the post -- Cold War trend of mayhem in the Pacific demands investigation into the media-politics-conflict nexus in the Pacific context . Do n't Spoil My Beautiful @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Singh is head of journalism at theUniversity of the South Pacific . " <p> Trawling has had a significant impact on the fished and unfished seamounts . <p> " Now , it 's more about asking , ' Are there enough coral eco-systems that are still there that can provide refuge for fish ? ' A lot of life is dependant on reef , " Tracey says . <p> She does n't believe banning trawling would be reasonable , however . " There are just some areas that need to be protected . @@13666577 <p> Global heat , greenhouse gases and sea levels all climbed to record highs last year , making 2015 the worst in modern times across a range of key environmental indicators , international scientists said yesterday . <p> A dire picture of the Earth 's health is painted in the State of the Climate report , a peer-reviewed 300-page tome that comes out once a year and is compiled by 450 scientists from around the world . <p> The record heat that the planet experienced last year was driven partially by global warming , and was exacerbated by the ocean-heating trend known as El Nino , it said . <p> El Nino , which ended last month , was one of the strongest the Earth had seen " since at least 1950 " , said the report , led by the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration ( NOAA ) National Centres for Environmental Information . <p> Thomas Karl , director of the NOAA division , described the report as an " annual physical " of the Earth 's health . <p> " Clearly , the report in 2015 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ increasing , but all the related symptoms that you might expect to see with a rising temperature are also occurring , " he said . <p> " El Nino certainly gave it a boost , so to speak , from the standpoint of global temperatures . " <h> Related Content <p> All three " rose to new record high values during 2015 " , said the findings , based on tens of thousands of measurements from multiple independent datasets . <p> The annual average atmospheric CO2 concentration at Mauna Loa , Hawaii , reached 400.8 parts per million ( ppm ) , surpassing 400 ppm for the first time , marking " the largest annual increase observed in the 58-year record " . On average globally , last year 's CO2 level was 399.4 ppm , an increase of 2.2 ppm over 2014 . <p> This " means that 2016 is easily going to surpass this milestone " , said climatologist Jessica Blunden , lead editor at NOAA 's National Centres for Environmental Information . <p> The report also confirmed the NOAA and Nasa finding that Earth 's average land and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Karl and Blunden said experts foresee this year setting a new record for global heat . <p> " Just because the El Nino has ended does not mean that we are going to go back to where we were before . We are going to continue to climb , " said Blunden . <p> Global sea levels swelled to their highest point yet , about 7cm higher than the 1993 average . <p> Sea level is creeping up gradually around the globe , averaging about 3.3cm a year , according to the report . In some places in the western Pacific and Indian Ocean waters rise faster . <p> Even though the current pace may appear slow , experts warn that sea-level rise will accelerate in the coming decades as glaciers and polar ice caps melt , putting millions of lives at risk in coastal communities around the world . <p> More extreme weather was seen in 2015 , too , with an above-normal rainy season prompting major floods in some parts of the world . <p> Meanwhile , areas in severe drought nearly doubled , from 8 per cent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year . <p> The Arctic , which is considered particularly sensitive to climate change , continued to warm , and increasing temperatures led to thinner and smaller sea ice cover . <p> " The Arctic land surface temperature tied with 2007 and 2011 as the highest since records began in the early 20th century , representing a 2.8C increase since that time , " said the report . <p> The Antarctic was colder than average , and the influence of El Nino on atmospheric circulation helped shift sea-ice cover " from record high levels in May to record low levels in August " , it said . <p> Just because the El Nino has ended does not mean that we are going to go back to where we were before . We are going to continue to climb . <p> Jessica Blunden <p> Across the globe , alpine glaciers continued to retreat for the 36th year in a row . <p> June 's late spring snow cover in the Northern Hemisphere marked the second-lowest in the 49-year satellite record . <p> Warming waters are also blamed for the severity of a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ California to British Columbia , Canada , resulting in " significant impacts to marine life , coastal resources and the human communities that depend on these resources " . <p> The Atlantic hurricane season was unusually mild for the second year in a row , largely due to El Nino , but tropical cyclones " were well above average overall " , said the report . <p> There were 101 tropical cyclones across all ocean basins last year , well above the 1981-2010 average of 82 storms . <p> The eastern and central Pacific was roiled by 26 big storms , the most since 1992.

@@13686182 <h> U-turn on climate change policy <p> Australia 's re-elected conservative government has announced a U-turn in its climate change policy , reinstating climate science as the bedrock of its peak science body just months after slashing its funding and axing hundreds of jobs . <p> Malcolm Turnbull <p> " It 's a new government and we 're laying out a direction that climate science matters , " new Science Minister Greg Hunt told Australian radio yesterday . <p> Scientists and climate change advocates cautiously welcomed the news , but remained concerned about the commitment to fight climate change , which could threaten Australia 's food security and its ability to feed Asia 's growing , affluent middle class . <p> Severe cuts to the Commonwealth Science and Industrial Research Organisation ( CSIRO ) climate change division were announced in February as a result of budget cuts imposed by the previous climate change sceptic prime minister , Tony Abbott . <p> Prime Minister Malcolm Turnbull , who toppled Abbott in a party-room coup in late 2015 and who has championed action against climate change , was elected last @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 15 new climate science jobs and research investment worth $A37 million ( $NZ39 million ) over 10 years , but scientists and advocates remained concerned about the CSIRO . <p> " We hope this is the start of more positive leadership from the Turnbull government on the critical threat of climate change , " Greenpeace Australia Pacific 's senior climate and energy campaigner Nikola Casule said . <p> " However , around 35 climate science jobs remain at risk , so restoring just 15 means the department is still under-resourced compared to earlier this year . " <p> Australia is one of the world 's largest exporters of coal and iron ore and one of its biggest carbon emitters on a per capita basis thanks to its reliance on coal-fired power plants . <p> Canberra pledged to cut emissions by 26-28% of 2005 levels by 2030 ahead of the Paris climate talks late last year but questions have been raised about its commitment to fulfilling that pledge .

Adding to the increased risk of waterborne disease that rising sea and rainfall levels can bring , Benschop says increased temperatures can also enhance the growth of bacteria that make us sick . <p> " Looking at what 's happening with these diseases now and looking at the Niwa prediction , these kind of diseases are expected to increase , " she says . <p> Possums and rats are expected to survive more easily in a warmer winter and come back in force during spring . <p> The Niwa prediction Benschop talks of is a report released in June which breaks down climate change effects for every region of New Zealand and pin-points some of the effects Taranaki is likely to experience in the next @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ call of droughts , increased rainfall , storms , cyclones , floods , landslides and erosion and an average temperature increase of 0.7 to 3.0 degrees Celsius . <p> The report , discussed by Taranaki Regional Councillors in July , was soberly received though one comment that climate change could put Taranaki in a good place for grape growing was not appreciated by many outside of council chambers . <p> Rob Tucker <p> Erosion like this beneath Point Rd in Mokau could be a more common sight along Taranaki 's coastline . <p> But , to be fair , climate change is not all doom and gloom . Niwa 's report states , for instance , the expected warmer temperatures could bring about a drop in cold-related illnesses as people spend less time indoors . <p> " It 's more about a change in human behaviour during colder weather , people spend more time together , " Benschop says . <p> " Those person to person diseases are more likely to spread in those situations . " <p> Robert Charles/Fairfax NZ <p> Severe flooding and water damage in the Waitotora Valley @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ But like with all upsides , there is a downside . The drop in incidences of flu , bronchial infections and common colds could be offset by the rise of sub-tropical diseases never seen here before . <p> Benschop says while New Zealand does n't have some of the mosquitoes responsible for diseases like dengue fever or the zika virus it 's " becoming more of a place where those mosquitoes could thrive " . <p> Researchers in Brazil have recently found the culex mosquito , a mosquito New Zealand does have , can carry the zika virus where previously Zika has thought to have only been transmitted by the Aedes aegypt mosquito species . <p> Benschop 's team have also seen cases where diseases like leptospirosis - which can cause bleeding from the lungs and meningitis - have been transmitted by rats and possums . <p> This could become a pressing issue because the predicted 1.0C to 3.0C temperature rise would see more pests surviving the winter , says Department of Conservation spokesman Herb Christophers . <p> " Invasive weeds , insects and mammalian pests will all potentially increase in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " If snow retreats it creates more accessible terrain for species less adapted to the cold . " <p> Christophers also points out that wasps would be able to survive a milder winter and swarm back in force during spring , an issue already prevalent in recent Taranaki summers . <p> But the warmth will also bring opportunity with the Niwa reporting stating climate change could bring a longer copping season , as well as the potential for new crops to be grown in areas previously unsuitable . <p> Unfortunately for some , Massey University agriculture and environment lecturer Huub Kerckhoffs does n't see great potential for grapes in Taranaki but kiwifruit and pip fruit trees such as apples and pears could thrive . <p> " If the breeding of plants can keep up with climate change then farmers might have a shot at adapting , " he says <p> But such opportunity would need significant infrastructural investment to make it profitable , he cautions . <p> " I think people just need to be aware of the whole system , not just one component like temperature . " <p> A @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plants will reach maturity much quicker in warmer temperatures , Kerckhoffs says . <p> " However , this could mean they 're not developing as they should and growers might have quality issues . " <p> And then there is the other issue that growers taking advantage of a warmer climate would also have to keep in mind a higher chance of freak weather events , says Niwa principal scientist Chris Brandolino . <p> For every 1.0C rise in temperature there will be an 8.0C per cent rise in rainfall intensity , he says . <p> " Things like tropical cyclones would be stronger and be able to cause a lot more damage . <p> " But it 's the extreme rainfall events which would likely be a big deal . " <p> Taranaki has already had a taste of what extreme rainfall and cyclones can do . <p> In 1988 the region was devastated by Cyclone Bola which not only tore off countless roofs and uprooted thousands of trees , it single handedly destroyed Taranaki burgeoning kiwi fruit industry . <p> In June 2015 South Taranaki was subject to extreme @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ residential areas in one weekend . <p> Climate change predictions have the gaps between such events getting ever smaller . <p> One of the affected farmers in 2015 was South Taranaki district councillor Ian Armstrong , who says infrastructure and preparation would save farms in the inevitable storms the future will bring . <p> " Widening rivers and raising culverts to deal with high rainfall would be a step in the right direction , " he says . <p> But despite evidence freak floods like last year will be less freak and more run-of-the-mill and need to be planned for sooner rather than later , farmers have more immediate things to worry about , he says . <p> " At the moment climate change falls by the wayside due to the low dairy payout . <p> " Longer term that might be something we worry about , but the payout takes centre stage for most us at the moment . <p> " Survival from one month to the next is more important for most . " <p> Central government dictates that local government must have a regard to the effects of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ word here is " effects " . It has no responsibility to stop climate change , just manage how it impacts their territories . <p> Taranaki Regional Council director for resource management Fred McLay explains it as " if someone wants to build a house on eroding shoreline we manage their resource consent , " but it 's not up to them to prevent the reasons the shoreline is eroding . <p> " It 's a central government issue to deal with the cause by managing things like emissions , " he says . <p> Except even if they are doing that in a sustainable way , it 's already too late to avoid climate change happening right now , says Niwa principal scientist Brett Mullan . <p> " If we stop putting carbon dioxide into the atmosphere , or conversely put more in , it hardly matters for the next 20 years , " he says . <p> " The atmosphere responds slowly to what we put in , we 've already committed to what 's in there now . @@13720952 <h> Breaking News <h> Global warming could lead to toxic waste leak <p> Global warming could release radioactive waste stored in an abandoned Cold War-era US military camp deep under Greenland 's ice caps if a thaw continues to spread in coming decades . <p> Camp Century was built in northwest Greenland in 1959 as part of US research into the feasibility of nuclear missile launch sites in the Arctic , the University of Zurich said in a statement on Friday . <p> Staff left gallons of fuel and an unknown amount of low-level radioactive coolant there when the base shut down in 1967 on the assumption it would be entombed forever , according to the university . <p> It is all currently about 35 metres down . <p> But the part of the ice sheet covering the camp could start to melt by the end of the century on current trends , the scientists added . <p> " Climate change could remobilise the abandoned hazardous waste believed to be buried forever beneath the Greenland ice sheet , " the university said of findings published this week in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ led by York University in Canada in collaboration with the University of Zurich , estimated that pollutants in the camp included 200,000 litres of diesel fuel and the coolant from a nuclear generator used to produce power . <p> " It 's a new breed of political challenge we have to think about , " lead author William Colgan , a climate and glacier scientist at York University , said in a statement . <p> " If the ice melts , the camp 's infrastructure , including any remaining biological , chemical , and radioactive wastes , could re-enter the environment and potentially disrupt nearby ecosystems , " the University of Zurich said . <p> The study said it would be extremely costly to try to remove any waste now . <p> It recommended waiting " until the ice sheet has melted down to almost expose the wastes before beginning site remediation. " 
@@13732472 <h> Quality debut novel for young adults <p> Australian author Mark Smith 's debut novel tells a beautiful and intimate story that belies the bleak future in which it is set . <p> It is is told through the eyes of 16-year-old Finn Morrison , the only person from his isolated coastal community to have survived waves of illness and violence that have killed most of Australia 's population . Thanks to his father 's foresight , he has enough food and fuel stockpiled to satisfy his basic needs for the meantime , and his life has settled into a familiar routine ; hunting , fishing and surfing , an activity which keeps him in touch with his old life and , by his own admission , sane . <p> But the only human contact he has had in two years is with an elderly man in the neighbouring valley , so when a terrified Afghani girl appears in the town , closely followed by a violent gang of men who control the land to the north of the settlement , he responds instinctively to protect her . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his home that Finn has time to fully consider the ramifications of his actions ; although the girl , Rose , who is ill and pregnant , is not herself a threat , by rescuing her he is in real danger of being discovered himself . <p> Her presence takes the edge off his loneliness , and after she describes her life growing up as a refugee and her treatment by the men now chasing her , he feels compelled to make up for all the bad things that have happened to her . So when she begs him to find her sister , from whom she became separated during their escape , he embarks on a second rescue mission , even though it forces him into direct conflict with her pursuers . <p> Although the basic plot elements of The Road to Winter are by no means new , what makes this novel stand out from the multitude of other dystopian near-futures that crowd bookshop shelves is Finn 's narrative voice . Although young , he is neither naive nor cynical , and , despite what he has lived through @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ immensely appealing . We get glimpses of the plague and its aftermath but no more than necessary to set the scene . <p> Smith also draws attention to important contemporary issues - Finn mentions how winters are growing briefer and milder due to climate change , and learns through Rose how asylum-seekers are forced into indentured labour , a policy euphemistically termed " refugee reassignment ' ' - but he does so with a similarly light touch . <p> Because of this focus on the characters at the story 's heart we become deeply invested in their welfare , and the main tension in the novel comes not from horrific details but from our desire to see them succeed . Like the best YA fiction , The Road to Winter is sure to appeal just as much to an adult audience , and I can easily see it being discussed as passionately by a local book club as a high-school English class . It is already being compared to John Marsden 's best-selling " Tomorrow ' ' series , in my opinion justly so .

@@13737340 <h> Data centre water use has investors on high alert <p> To keep from overheating , large data centers pump hundreds of millions of gallons of water a year through the facilities . Photo / Getty Images <p> Data centres , used by governments and large corporations to house their computer systems , have one big environmental problem : They get hot . <p> To keep them from overheating , large data centres can pump hundreds of millions of gallons of water a year through the facilities , according to company reports . That high demand for water has some investors concerned , especially in places where natural water resources are becoming ever more precious , like tech-heavy California . <p> " We definitely want our portfolio companies to be cognisant of their water use and take the appropriate steps to minimise their water use and recycle water , " said Brian Rice , portfolio manager at the California State Teachers ' Retirement System , which manages about $189 billion in assets as of June 30 . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as well as at other portfolio companies , such as those in agriculture . <p> California-home to companies running some of the world 's biggest data centres-houses more than 800 of the facilities , the most of any state , according to Dan Harrington , research director of 451 Research , a technology consulting firm . <p> Water usage there is especially a concern as the state 's drought pushes into its fifth year . <p> California Gov . Jerry Brown issued an executive order in May to extend statewide emergency water restrictions , establishing long-term measures to conserve water . <p> The water risk to investors of California-based companies operating data centres will not affect them gradually , said Julie Gorte , senior vice president of sustainable investing at Pax World Management . <p> As a result , some sustainable-minded investors are trying to enhance their understanding of water risk before it becomes a liability , said Cate Lamb , head of water at investor environmental advocacy group CDP . <p> The group held a series of workshops this year for investors to discuss their most crucial water reporting needs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The number of institutional investors committed to its water engagement program with companies has grown to 617 from 150 in 2010 . <p> Operational efficiencies at data centres have a direct link to companies ' profitability and pose an increasing risk for investors in a " tense " climate change environment , said Himani Phadke , research director at the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board , a non-profit that writes corporate sustainability reporting guidelines for investors . <p> Companies , like investors , are trying to get ahead of the risk . <p> Bill Weihl , director of sustainability at Facebook , said the company uses a combination of fresh air and water to cool its data centres . @@13770006 <h> Ten things to know about climate change <p> A Victoria University image shows what could happen to sea-level in Wellington if average temperatures rise by 2 degrees Celsius this century compared with pre-industrial levels . <p> New Zealand is a leading climate researcher but when it comes to doing something about climate change we lag behind other countries . PROFESSOR JAMES RENWICK explains . <p> In terms of climate research , New Zealand is right up there . <p> We are home to the longest-running observational record for carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) concentrations in the Southern Hemisphere and New Zealand scientists are doing world-leading work on the effects of climate change , from extreme rainfalls to the fate of the Antarctic ice sheets . <p> Carbon dioxide in the air is like a blanket on our bed , keeping us warm underneath . More CO2 ( and other so-called " greenhouse gases " ) in the air means a thicker blanket and toastier conditions down where we live . <p> We know the build-up of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere comes from burning fossil fuels . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a sure sign of burning . <p> Meanwhile , the chemical signature of the carbon in the atmosphere is changing , as carbon that has been underground for millions of years ( i.e. oil and coal ) has a different chemical signature to the carbon we breathe out every day . So , it 's clear that burning of fossil fuels is responsible for the increase in the concentration of CO2 in the atmosphere . <p> Experiments with climate models show that only by including greenhouse gas increases can we capture the rise in global temperatures over the past 60 or more years . Changes in the Sun 's output or other " natural " factors just do n't do it . <p> So , we know that we are responsible for the increase in atmospheric greenhouse gases and we know that increase is the cause of the climate changes we are seeing around us . <p> The first half of 2016 was the warmest start to a year on record , both in New Zealand and globally . <p> The Paris Agreement calls on the global community to keep @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pre-industrial temperatures ) and preferably nearer 1.5C . On an annual basis , the globe is already above 1C of warming and some months of this year have broken 1.5C , so we know that time is very short . <p> We have a maximum budget of CO2 emissions that we can not exceed if we are to keep global warming below 2C . At current rates , we will have blown that budget in 20 years , so significant reductions must start immediately . <p> Most of the extra warming coming from increased carbon dioxide is warming the oceans -- they absorb more than 90 per cent of the heat and 25% of the CO2 . This is good news in the sense that it slows the warming we feel , but it 's bad news because it 'll take a long time for the oceans to adjust and cool . <p> The deep southern oceans are warming rapidly and the warm water is nibbling away at the edges of the Antarctic ice sheets , exposing us to the risk of many metres of sea-level rise over coming centuries . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ) report of up to a metre by 2100 are likely to be under-estimates , because we understand better now just how sensitive the big ice sheets are to warming ocean water . While sea levels globally may not rise much more than 1-metre this century , we may be locking in 10m or even 20m more over the next several centuries , committing hundreds of millions of people to forced migration . <p> Changes to the average climate are felt most through changes in extreme events . In New Zealand , we can expect to see three or four times as many hot days by the end of the century , with 2-3C average warming . Drought frequency is likely to increase by a factor of two or three in eastern and northern regions , leading to water availability issues from Otago to Northland . Winter snow pack could decrease by 40 per cent , even in the highest parts of the Southern Alps . <p> One of the ways of thinking about climate change is that the world is becoming more tropical . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it is happening . One local consequence is that the " East Australian Current " that flows south off the coast of New South Wales and Victoria , carrying warm water out of the tropics , has been getting more intense and a lot warmer . This warms the western Tasman Sea and may help increase the warming of New Zealand , should the warm water start flowing east faster and farther south than it does now . <p> When it comes to tackling global warming , New Zealand is starting on the back foot . On a per-head basis , we emit way more greenhouse gases than the OECD average . <p> Given we have benefited from our use of fossil fuels , we have as much responsibility as any country to cut our emissions . The good news is we can make serious reductions right now , even despite our dependence on agriculture . <p> Getting to 100 per cent renewable electricity , boosting public transport and uptake of electric vehicles , cleaning up industrial use of energy , reducing waste going to landfills and just planting trees to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up to a 40 per cent reduction in national emissions by 2030 . <p> That would be a vastly better outcome for the climate than the present government policy of international trading to meet our targets . We are banking on other countries doing the job while we ride on their coat-tails . <p> Even as we reduce emissions , we must adapt to changes that are already happening . Coastal flooding and inundation will become more and more of an issue , along with water availability problems and increased coastal flooding . <p> As a small nation we lead in so many areas and we can also lead in making the zero-carbon economy a reality . The moral , social and economic benefits could be huge .

@@13792311 <p> Ovini Uera works for the country 's only airline , Nauru Airways , as a catering manager . <p> The population of the Olympic Village is around 18,000 , which means that technically it is the biggest place that Ovini Uera has ever lived in . His home country of Nauru has a population of about 10,000 , making it the smallest nation at these Games . <p> It has one hospital , one hotel and one supermarket . If you want to get somewhere , you can either walk ( only 19 kilometres all the way around ) or track down the island 's only taxi . <p> When he is not training in the country 's only judo gym , Uera works for the country 's only airline , Nauru Airways , as a catering manager . He used to be a flight attendant on the airline 's only plane . And now he is the only Nauru judoka ever to win a match at the Olympic Games . <p> ELSA/GETTY IMAGES <p> Flag bearer Elson Brechtefeld of Nauru leads his team during the Opening @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ country , but usually in the sense of being a smaller part of a greater enterprise . But what if , to all intents and purposes , you are the enterprise ? Uera makes up 50 per cent of Nauru 's Olympic team at these Games ; its other member , Elson Brechtefeld , got to carry the flag at the opening ceremony . Uera does not mind . He reckons he will get another chance soon enough . <p> Uera is at the Olympics on merit - he won a bronze medal at the recent Oceanic Games - but he only took up the sport four years ago . " Judo back home is really , really small , " he explains . " There 's only one gym , and a maximum of 20 judokas . I was studying boxing and Muay Thai , and needed to learn some ground-based arts : grappling , and throwing . That 's when I found judo , and fell in love with the sport . " <p> When you are from one of the world 's smallest countries you need to go @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ long way , in fact . " I had to make a lot of sacrifices , " Uera says . " I had to fly overseas for competitions and was going up against full-time athletes , which was really tough . I still have to do my job , go home and carry out my duties as a husband and father , and squeeze in a few judo sessions . " <p> Because this is the other thing about Nauru : not only is it small , but it is a very long way from anywhere else . Plonked down in the Pacific , around halfway between Hawaii and Australia , it is one of the most remote countries in the world . It is 321km to its closest neighbour , Banaba Island in Kiribati , but frankly you would simply be swapping one island in the middle of nowhere for another . It is 2896km to Australia , the country that has exerted a greater influence on Nauru 's fate than any other . <p> INVASION <p> The recent history of has not been a bed of palm leaves . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ recaptured by Australia during the First World War , then suffered an influenza epidemic that killed a fifth of its population . In the Second World War it was shelled by the Germans , occupied by the Japanese and then bombed by the Americans . But the good times were coming . <p> After it gained independence in 1968 , mining companies poured into the country , attracted by the island 's rich phosphate reserves . Almost overnight , Nauru became one of the world 's richest countries . <p> But when , inevitably , the phosphate began to run out , it went into steep decline . By the turn of the century , it was virtually bankrupt . The entire centre of the island had been ravaged by strip mining and was virtually unusable . Unemployment was at 90 per cent . Corruption and money-laundering festered . Climate change was wrecking its fishing industry . <p> And so when the Australian government offered it a sackful of cash in 2001 to host an offshore asylum processing centre , it had no choice but to accept . So until 2008 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ best known as an auxiliary piece in Australia 's toxic immigration debate . <p> It is why Nauru looks to events like the Olympics : to tell the world a different story about itself , an alternative to the received tale of penury and pain . Weightlifting is its traditional strength - its 10 Commonwealth Games golds put it ahead of Zimbabwe , Sri Lanka and Hong Kong - but Uera is hoping that a nation inspired by his feats can begin to make waves in judo , too . <p> Against Renick James of Belize in the men 's 90kg , he makes history . His signature move - there is probably a technical term , but let 's just call it sticking your rear end into the other bloke and trying to flip him over your back - proves wildly successful . He loses to his Georgian opponent in the last 16 , but the Uera that emerges from the arena , breathless and glistening with sweat , could scarcely be happier . <p> UNDERDOG <p> " For Nauru , we 're always the underdog , " he smiles @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 're dedicated to something , you 'll do whatever it takes . We 're honoured and happy to show off our country . Right now it 's 3am , so I 'm keeping the whole island up . " <p> There is a Commonwealth Games in Australia to come in 2018 , and another Olympics in 2020 . But for now , he is required back at work . Not that he is bitter about it . " I 've got a full-time job back on the island , a pretty wife and a lovely daughter , " he says . And as he walks off to get showered and changed , you would swear you were looking at the proudest man in Brazil .

@@13797457 <h> When will it be too hot to hold the Summer Olympics ? <p> Heat stress due to climate change will limit where and when the summer Olympics can be held in the future , according to new research from the University of Auckland . <p> The commentary study , published in the British medical journal , The Lancet this week , looked at the impact of increased temperatures on Northern Hemisphere countries ' ability to stage the iconic Summer Olympics ' marathon . <p> " High-visibility international athletic events such as the Summer Olympics represent just a small fraction of heavy exertion outdoors , " says co-author , Professor Alistair Woodward head of the University 's department of Epidemiology and Biostatistics . <p> " But increasing restrictions on when , where , and how the Games can be held owing to extreme heat are a sign of a much bigger problem . <p> " If the world 's most elite athletes need to be protected from climate change , what about the rest of us ? " he says . <p> By 2085 the study finds almost @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be too hot and humid during the summer months to safely run the marathon . <p> " Only three cities in North America , two in Asia and none in Africa will fall in the low risk category , " says Professor Woodward . " Projections to early next century suggest the last cities with low-risk summer conditions will be Belfast , Dublin , Edinburgh and Glasgow . " <p> The study was in collaboration with colleagues from the University of California , the Nelson Marlborough Institute of Technology , and the Centre for Technology Research and Innovation , at Limassol in Cyprus . <p> " Climate change threatens human health in many ways - through heat waves , extreme weather events , and shifts in disease vectors , as well as economic and social stresses on populations living in or trying to escape areas affected by seawater intrusion , drought , lower agricultural productivity , and floods . " <p> " In the short term , most of these impacts could be lessened , such as by actions to reduce background disease risks and other known causes of vulnerability , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2050 poses increasingly difficult challenges , not only because of the inherent uncertainties in long- term predictions , but because the extent and speed of change might exceed society 's ability to adapt . <p> " The risk of so-called pernicious impacts -- those that require trade-offs between what is generally assumed and valued as part of society and what is healthy -- will rise , " he says . <p> Professor Woodward suggested that perhaps the most pernicious of these impacts was the growing expansion in season and geography of outdoor conditions ( or unprotected indoor spaces ) in which heavy work is no longer safe . <p> " As more than half the planet 's workforce works outdoors , primarily in construction and agriculture , society faces an increasingly serious trade-off between population health and labour productivity , " he says . <p> " The risk to workers ' health could be minimised if workers are allowed to sit in the shade during the hottest times of day and take breaks during hot , humid months . <p> " Otherwise , exertional heat stroke and its negative outcomes , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ work around the world , " says Professor Woodward . <p> " Increasingly , people will face a choice between doing what they have done for millions of years -- work hard outdoors essentially any time they wish -- and being safe . " <p> He says that heavy work outdoors is already limited in some parts of the world by heat stress -- as measured by the wet- bulb globe temperature ( WBGT ) , a combination of temperature , humidity , heat radiation , and wind -- and climate change means more regions will be affected for a greater part of the year . <p> The Reserve Bank wo n't impose new lending restrictions for highly-leveraged investment purchases until the start of October , giving lenders an extra month to clear their backlog of pre-approvals . @@13805620 <p> Generation Z has been born into a world in which life , in many ways , sucks.In 10 years ' time , the younger members of this generation will be leaving university , almost certainly more heavily indebted than any generation before them , and they will be entering a workplace in which it is estimated that half of all currently existing jobs will have disappeared . The jobs that still exist will be increasingly insecure . <p> Gen Z-ers can expect to have eight major job changes over their working lifetimes.Precluding a housing crash , only the thinnest scraping of Gen Z-ers -- those with excellent jobs and with wealthy , benevolent parents -- are going to be able to buy a house in Auckland . Even then , those houses will most likely be on the bare edge of the city , miles of car-clogged road from the love and support of their families . <p> " Generation Z " is one of those sloppy terms , co-opted by different people and groups to indicate different things . Roughly though , the first of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the mid-2000s , now ranging in age from their mid-20s down to those still in primary school . <p> They will emerge into a job market more precarious and changeable than any before , with more student debt than any generation before them , facing the likelihood of never owning a home in the city in which they grew up . Then there 's climate change , rampant inequality , Trumpism and so forth . <p> " There 's quite a generational tension that operates at the moment , " says Paul Spoonley , distinguished professor at Massey University , " that older generations have had the state look after their education or their health or their superannuation , and I think the younger generations are now at the point that they accept they 're probably going to have to pay for a lot more of this , that nobody is going to look after them in the way that previous generations have been looked after . And so , I think they 're more cynical . " <p> Cynicism is fine and probably helpful in the situation they 've been @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Psychologists say that today 's young people are less resilient than ever . Teachers both here and overseas are reporting increases in student depression , anxiety and decreases in their ability to deal with everyday problems . Leading American psychologist Peter Gray talks of a major university he was working with , at which a student reported being traumatised when her roommate called her " bitch " and two students sought counselling after seeing a mouse in their house . <p> Continued below . <h> Related Content <p> Part of the reason for this decline in resilience , says Elizabeth Peterson , senior lecturer in psych-ology at the University of Auckland , is over-protective parenting , which prevents them from learning how to deal with the emotions that come with setbacks . It creates an environment in which it 's not safe to make mistakes . <p> " If it 's not safe to make mistakes , " she says , " it 's not safe to grow . " <p> While the age of puberty varies around the world , it 's been steadily lowering , and is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ often now go through puberty about age 10 or 11 , with boys typically about a year later . Paediatricians have noticed an increase in children going through early puberty , which is now defined as before 8 in girls and before 9 in boys . At the same time , the age at which young people 's ability to reason is fully developed is getting later , creating in that gap what Peterson calls a " double whammy " : hormones leading to erratic behaviour , combined with a brain that does n't how to deal with that . <p> Issues of resilience are on the mind of Hobsonville Point Secondary School principal Maurie Abraham , and they are issues that the school has to some extent been built on . <p> " We can make it harder if we protect them too much , " Abraham says , " or if schools are too institutionalised . What we do is grizzle about how much resilience the young people do n't have and how they 're not independent learners but we make them go to a school where a bell @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and when , we tell them what to learn and when to learn it and if they try to break out of that , we grab them and punish them and chuck them back into the machine again right up until they 're 18 . No way is that preparing them for the world . " <p> Abraham and three Hobsonville Point students sat down with me one recent afternoon and discussed what it meant to be part of Generation Z. " I think our generation is at a turning point , " Year 11 student Meike Van Roij , 15 , said . " Everything leading up to this , we 're almost the ones that need to make the switch . " <p> " I use an analogy , " her classmate Flynn Dawson said . " Think of somebody born in 1890 or 1900 , you would think of them as the same person because they 've grown up in the same environment ; whereas you think of someone born in 1990 or 2000 , like us , you would think of them as completely different people because @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so drastically . " <p> He went on : " We 're just trying to cope with it as best we can . " <p> At the heart of this change is technology , specifically the ability to connect all the time with everyone in the world , the connection through mobile devices to Snapchat , Instagram , Facebook , to never be away from people , to never be alone . <p> Technology , obviously , brings its own challenges and potential problems , but it 's the least of their worries . Are they angry or resentful towards previous generations for the damage they have done to the climate , to the housing market , to their chances of ever emerging from under a debt mountain to live decent , secure lives ? <p> " All these things have happened , " Dawson said , " but obviously in the 70s and 80s , they did n't know that . People did n't know about climate change until ... slightly earlier , I think it was brought up but ... " <p> " Every generation is going to leave @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ were talking before about the benefits of social media and also there 's going to be negatives . Everything is going to have a negative and positive as long as you want to keep advancing and that 's something we naturally do . " <p> " I think we have to go back a bit further to find the people to blame , to be honest , " classmate Angus Lynch said . <p> Are they angry towards those people ? <p> " Nah , " they all said together . <p> " Because to them , " Dawson said , " the people who made the first steam engine or something , a massive advancement , it 's obviously changed our world in both positive and negative ways . " <p> " And we have no idea , " Lynch said . " We could be doing the same thing . We could make all these solar panels ... " <p> " We could invent AI in a couple of years , " Dawson interjected . <p> " What happens if we do solar panels , " Lynch said , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ electricity with solar panels and we discover in 100 years that solar panels , for whatever reason , do something ? " <p> Are they optimistic ? <p> " I think it 's based on the way we go now from here on out , " Dawson said . " Based on what we do , it 's going to have an impact on the world -- positive or negative . If we can find a way to fix these problems we have now , we can focus on making the world better . " <p> They demand a voice . I 'd say that 's the key difference . And we fail to meet their needs when we do n't listen to their voice . <p> Maurie Abraham <p> Hobsonville Point Secondary School is an interesting case study because it is arguably Auckland 's newest and most radical answer to one of the big questions of generational change : if we accept both that young people and the world around them are changing , do we need to change the way we educate those young people or do young people @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Maurie Abraham says , " You 've got to be prepared to throw out the tried and true methods that have been in place for more than half a century . Places like Auckland Grammar and Epsom Girls ' Grammar have always been sought-after but many parents are also looking to Catholic schools for their conservative values and for their academic achievements that are often wildly superior to other schools from equivalent areas and deciles . <p> St Paul 's College , a Catholic school in Ponsonby , is what you might call a traditional school . " <p> Abraham says : " We 're not purposefully setting out to change the world but we 're wanting to grow people who want to make the world better . " <p> It is not a new thing to talk about the future of the world in apocalyptic terms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the one we 're in now -- it can be tempting , and even strangely comforting . But the world is not doomed , at least not yet . <p> " If we were talking in the middle of the agrarian revolution or the industrial revolution , " Spoonley says , " we 'd probably be saying the world 's coming to an end and the outlook for our children is hopeless . What happens is a new world is created . It 's just not clear exactly what the shape of that new world is . " <p> Meike Van Roij , Flynn Dawson and Angus Lynch . Photo / Greg Bowker <h> Z speak ... <p> The hand-picked students of Hobsonville Point Secondary School , absurdly bright , practically vibrating with insight and ambition , sit opposite their principal , Maurie Abraham - " Maurie " they call him , as do all their schoolmates - while I ask for insights into what it 's like to be of their generation . They do their best to answer for the widely diverse range of people who make up that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " . <p> They tell me their classes last as long as 160 minutes - " half a day , basically " - and are mostly taught as " modules " , with names like " Pandemic " , " Architects " and " iCare " rather than as " subjects " with names like " maths " and " English " , although Maurie strongly insists the school is driven by the standard New Zealand curriculum . <p> One of the things we think Generation Z demand is that they have to see the relevance of the learning otherwise they 're not going to bother . <p> Maurie Abraham <p> The students speak about how they spend each Wednesday working on big projects of their own devising , which can last for months or even years , whatever they want , really Fifteen-year-old Meike Van Roij says she spends her Wednesdays working on a project investigating new ways to deal with antibiotic resistance . She 's working with two PhD students and a researcher from the University of Auckland . She anticipates the project will take three years - the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It 's obviously a really big thing , " she says . " I " ve only just started researching it and that 's taken half a year . " <p> Another of the students , Angus Lynch , has spent the first half of the year working on a project to develop a self-healing material . A Truth and Reconciliation Commission @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " . <p> It is late afternoon at Iqaluit ? on Baffin Island in the far north of Canada . In August , you can expect highs of 10 degrees Celsius and overnight lows of 4C or 5C . Which sounds entirely liveable . But winter is another story . When it is cold , you typically might get a low of minus 31 degrees Celsius and a high of -24C . <p> Watt-Cloutier grew up a little further south , in an Inuit community called Kuujjuaq ? in northern Quebec . So much of her work as a writer and activist for her Inuit people has been about the assessment of rapid change on these once isolated places . <p> " I travelled only by dog team for the first 10 years of my life , " she says . " We lived very traditionally in those years and in my one lifetime , we have to come to the modern world . We travel by snowmobiles in the winter and much faster powered boats than the canoes I used to travel in as a kid . It has taken @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to come from that place of hunter , gatherer , fisher to where we as Inuit have gone in one lifetime . " <p> Her snappy way of describing it in her recent book The Right to be Cold is that the Inuit have gone from the ice age to the space age in her lifetime . And it has exacted a toll . <p> " It is not just the speed at which it 's happened . There have been a lot of what we call historical traumas and I suspect there is common history with many other indigenous people , perhaps even in New Zealand and Australia . People were forcefully relocated in various areas of the High Arctic . Many children were sent away at a very young age for education , in a sense to be deprogrammed and reprogrammed . As president of the council , she linked indigenous human rights to the threat of climate change . What happens when your culture is based on being cold but everyone else in the world is making it warmer ? " That really took a toll on their dignity and the ability of hunters to provide for their families in a transitioning world .
