@@13828459 <h> Conservation Comment : Sceptics trying to trump science <p> AS MOST of us are aware , United States party conventions have been taking place over recent weeks . Among the many revelations has been Donald Trump announcing again that climate change is a " hoax " . <p> Like most of his statements , no evidence is produced -- they are the " shoot from the mouth " variety . But apparently all but one of the 19 original contenders for the Republican nomination actually agree with him . <p> It seems extraordinary to me that so many political leaders ( of a particular persuasion ) can fly in the face of the overwhelming scientific evidence to the contrary . For example , they can apparently trust the the scientists of Nasa to land a rocket on a rock out in space , yet disbelieve the same scientists when they confirm human-created climate change , with the mountains of evidence they have gathered . <p> English physicist Brian Cox put it this way : " Being an expert does not mean that you are someone with a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ something . <p> You 're not necessarily right , but you 're more likely to be right than someone who has n't spent their life studying it . " <p> A researcher at the University of Canterbury has recently gone so far as to say that " the term ' climate sceptic ' is now interchangeable with the term ' mindless fool ' . It ought to be seen as a crime ! " <p> I 'm sure that our local sceptics and deniers would assert the reverse to be true . It is we who are the fools -- even though we have a huge body of scientific opinion on our side . <p> So what is going on ? With all the evidence -- which grows by the day -- why is there still so much denial and so little action ? <p> Some of the explanations might include the following : It 's too big to contemplate , so best to ignore or deny it . Facing it requires too big a shift in our current way of doing things . It clashes too starkly with our personal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been wrong before , so they could be now . Doing something will cost too much . We just need to be patient -- science will come up with an answer . <p> Perhaps one of the biggest problems is that the reality of climate change is so often portrayed in such catastrophic terms . We 're all doomed ! We should know by now that scaring people usually turns them off . So we have to give people something to look forward to , some light at the end of the tunnel . We also need to understand that if we want the facts of climate change to be accepted we must realise that people interpret those facts according to their values -- what 's most important to them . How the facts are presented , including the solutions , must include how everyone gets something out of the deal . Finally , those of us who are trying to lead the change must demonstrate the change ourselves ( for example , stop flying , or give up our cars ) . Too often we do n't and we , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ convenient and comfortable . <p> It is becoming pretty obvious that there has been enough talk . Change requires action . <p> ? Philip McConkey has worked in the care professions most of his life , as a social worker , counsellor and family therapist . He is the father of three daughters and has five grandchildren . He is active in the Green Party because he accords with its values .

@@13837146 <h> Warming as real as your blankets <p> Hi Anne . I 'd be interested in the source of your quote that , " a while ago 97 percent of scientists believed the world was flat . . . " <p> Since ancient times Greeks have observed lunar eclipses and noted the circular shape of the Earth 's shadow on the full moon , and correctly surmised that the Earth was a globe . Indeed Eratosthenes , the chief librarian at Alexandria , fairly accurately calculated the circumference of the Earth in 240 BC . I enjoyed investigating this as part of my Masters of Science ( Astronomy ) . The flat-Earth belief being recent is something of an " old wives tale " . <p> Regarding global warming , it 's as real as the blankets on the readers ' beds . Indeed , if we did n't have the greenhouse effect , the average temperature of our planet would be -- 18 degrees Celsius ( note the minus ) . <p> Thankfully carbon from life forms , sedimentary rock and other sources are released into the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as is H2O ) . This CO2 ( and other gases ) release into the atmosphere provides an insulating layer that lets in solar radiation during the day but prevents some heat from escaping at night -- providing Earth with an average temperature of +14C . <p> Unfortunately , since the industrial revolution the amount of carbon dioxide released into our atmosphere by natural processes has been eclipsed by artificial means by over 135 times . <p> Put another way , humans produce more CO2 in three days than do all the volcanoes on Earth in one year ! <p> Bob Hughes ( Wrong priorities for today , August 10 letter ) is correct in stating that , " climate change ( is ) not a joke " ( although I am very pro-tourism for our unique slice of paradise ) . <p> Insofar as I 'm concerned , global warming is real -- people of course have a right to be sceptics . <p> However , is n't it more mature as guardians of our planet to be concerned with the amount of artificial by-products being released into our one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of ( as some do ) shrugging the shoulders and saying that they do n't believe that ( artificial ) global warming exists or has an effect ? <p> John Drummond <p> President , Gisborne Astronomical Society <p> Re : Sceptics due a lynching ? @@13838507 <p> New Zealanders take lot of pride in their national culture . And rightly so . <p> New Zealand is one of the few settler cultures lucky enough to have tangata whenua establish a treaty with it based on mutuality , reciprocity and respect . It 's a culture based on a strong sense of equality and " fair go " , one that values humility and has that ethos of looking out for your neighbour . <p> But that does n't mean NZ always lives up to these values . <p> Just look at the ongoing Crown betrayals of te Tiriti , the endemic male violence , the deepening poverty and inequality and the Government 's inertia against grave crises we face today such as homelessness , housing , and runaway climate change . <p> But these values are a strong part of our history . They 're core to our culture . <p> The cultural history of strong women leaders and of being the first to give women the vote . Of young Pacific and pakeha people standing up against the dawn raids in the 1970s @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in South Africa . Of leading the world in creating a welfare state that looked after the most vulnerable in society . And of political leaders in international arenas standing up for human rights . <p> West Papua 's on the western side of New Guinea , bordering Papua New Guinea . Its indigenous people are n't Asians , they 're Melanesians - just like their cousins in PNG and just like me . However , they 're not independent like PNG . They were once occupied by the Dutch and for the last 54 years they 've been occupied by Indonesia . <p> West Papuans have always wanted independence , but when the Dutch pulled out Indonesia saw a chance to occupy West Papua to grab all the mineral wealth and natural resources it has . And it 's got a lot : gold , copper , tin -- you name it . It 's so rich I call it " The Africa of the South Pacific " . So , when the Dutch finally pulled out , Indonesia -- backed by the United States -- saw its chance to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . And it 's been occupying West Papua ever since . <p> West Papuans have lost most of their land and seen more than 500,000 people killed . Women have been routinely abused and raped . People 's houses are burnt by militias and land is being poisoned , stripped and destroyed by extractive mining and industrial agriculture with entire villages being displaced . <p> Police arrest a man after dispersing hundreds of West Papuans attending a ceremony in Timika to commemorate the 50th anniversary of West Papua 's independence from Dutch rule in December 2011 . Photo : REUTERS <p> They 're being reduced to a minority population in their own land through Indonesian trans-migration . And they 're being routinely killed , thrown in jail , tortured and abused just for speaking out , resisting the occupation , and fighting for their independence . They ca n't fly their independence flag and they ca n't talk about independence for fear of reprisal . <p> I believe all these developments meet the definition of genocide . There is genocide going on next door . That alone should be enough for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reason . One we ignore at our peril . <p> NZ 's silence about West Papua is n't just about how we 're privileging our economic relationship with Indonesia over the human and indigenous rights of West Papuans ( NZ exports some $800 million to Indonesia every year and that figure 's expected to grow ) . <p> There 's a deeper moral issue here too as there 's an inseparable connection between how we deal with issues overseas and how we deal with issues in NZ . <p> If we tolerate injustice abroad , we 'll inevitably also undermine our own capacity to deal with injustice at home . It goes the other way too : if we tolerate injustices here , we 'll have less capacity to speak out against them over there . <p> Firstly , this means NZ 's silence over the genocide in West Papua is not unrelated to our collective inability to confront the ongoing realities of colonisation that Maori still experience here . <p> Secondly , if we do n't speak out about West Papua , one way or another it 'll undermine our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> These are problems like the corporate capture of mainstream media , increasing subordination of civil service to ministerial control , and the undermining of statutory bodies ' independence through threats of defunding . <p> The destruction of privacy through mass surveillance , passing a whole suite of acts post-9/11 that undermine the independence of the judiciary and breach the Bill of Rights , the long-standing corporate assault against unions , and the commodification of art and culture . <p> All of these are undermining the bulwarks of democracy in this country . All of which stem from the contradictions of colonisation here . <p> So speaking out about West Papua is n't just an obligation we have to look out for our neighbours . It 's also a chance to save ourselves , save NZ 's liberal democracy , and to honour te Tiriti , which means to actually live up to those values that so many Kiwis hold dear . <p> * Pala Molisa is an accounting lecturer at Victoria University whose research focuses on how social practices such as accounting help to create the conditions for human @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ member of the Run It Straight Collective , which has released the short film , Run It Straight , on the crisis in West Papua .

@@13852569 <h> Breaking News <h> The changing world as seen from space <p> New Zealand researchers are monitoring changes to the world 's major habitats using data collected from space . <p> The University of Otago team , including Professor Steven Higgins , has developed a system which they say could be used to study the effects of climate change on flora and fauna . <p> Using data collected by NASA satellites , they divided the world 's vegetation cover into 24 different ' biome ' types - producing a map that is n't " reliant on expert opinion or correlations between vegetation and climate " . <p> They found 13 percent of the world 's surface has changed from one biome type to another over the last 30 years , as climate change ramps up . <p> " Because it 's based on satellite observations and these satellites are up there all the time , we can then use this as a monitoring system which would allow us to detect areas where changes are going on , " says Prof Higgins . <p> " Substantial shifts in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Examples include swathes of Nambia and north-central Australia transitioning into drier biome categories and large expanses of cold limited systems shifting to more productive categories . " <p> He says the system will pave the way for future studies on the effects of climate change . <p> " Those data are available to anyone in the world , so someone sitting here down in Dunedin in New Zealand can do very powerful analyses which have global significance . " <p> He says future studies could use this system to pinpoint global warming as the reason for big changes . <p> " Our system provides an objective way to classify the land surface , which is important not only for monitoring change but also for comparing the behaviour of ecosystems in different parts of the world . "

@@13875229 <h> Walter Nash Labour Party Centennial Speech <p> One of the quotations from Sir Walter that I most admire is : ' I do n't want to get rid of poverty just to ensure that prosperity is maintained . I want to get rid of poverty because it is bad , it is wrong , it isimmoral , it is unethical , it is un-Christian , and it is unjust , and it is everything that is bad . I mean involuntary poverty -- where a man is told that his hands are not wanted , and that his wife and his youngsters will be deprived of the necessary things for health . ' <p> These words still ring with authenticity in New Zealand today . And I also remember the photo of him as our 78-year-old P.M. with only a majority of one in Parliament , boarding a plane to fly to the U.N . to help set up a new Department for Children 's Education -- now known as UNESCO -- such was his commitment to those less privileged -- and this is the legacy of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Labour Party and New Zealand should be proud of and have no hesitation in following . <p> But times , of course , as they always do , have changed . Politics , and too many politicians around the world , including New Zealand , have shown little regard for the real life issues faced by the average citizen . At one time , the causes of fairness , equal opportunity , full employment , decent and affordable housing , together with a free high quality education and health systems were shared goals by both politicians and the society they represented . This has not been the case for the last 30-40 years all around the world , including , unhappily , New Zealand . For example , for the first time in my lifetime an American political party -- thanks to Bernie Sanders -- has a more progressive policy platform than any political party in New Zealand ! <p> The election of Jeremy Corbyn and the campaign of Bernie Sanders , no matter what the immediate election results might show , represent a return to the politics of inclusion , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of a fair society . The outrageous and ever increasing gap between the rich and the poor , with the resulting crisis of child poverty and social dislocation , is increasingly seen as simply unacceptable to more and more people throughout the world . That is why many people have deserted political parties because they feel that political parties have deserted them and the lives they lead . In speaking in a straight forward manner about these issues and the solutions to them , Corbyn and Sanders have struck a chord with a wide audience . It is one which all political parties of the Left would do well to heed . <p> Of course , inequality in New Zealand has not just happened . The Guardian Weekly recently quoted an OECD report : ' in two decades from 1985 onwards , New Zealand had the biggest increase in income gaps of any developed country . Incomes for the richest Kiwis doubled , while those of the poorest stagnated ' . New Zealand is now just as divided by a wealth and income gap as the country that many of our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> One of the biggest problems faced by political parties is the temptation to see policy solutions as short-term . Walter Nash did not have that difficulty -- he always took the long view -- and the New Zealand Labour Party had a history of doing just that . Their early stance on the Vietnam War , Nuclear Weapons and Apartheid-based sport , are cases in point . <p> The Brexit vote was a clear example of short-termism and ignoring the lessons of history . Even if there was a short-term gain to the UK ( and I very much doubt it ) , where is the memory of a relatively peaceful Europe for over 70 years since 1945 compared to the cost in lives and development of two disastrous world wars in 20 years , between 1918 and 1939 , in a divided Europe ? <p> I want to say a few words on what a policy programme might look like for the future of a New Zealand which is fairer and more socially cohesive than it is at present . The first thing to say is that a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ago and since then Labour has failed to promote and defend the record of the Clark-led coalition government . From 2001 - 2008 economic growth ( GDP ) increased by a whopping 51.7% ( an average of 6.4% p.a. ) and across all regions of New Zealand . Since National took office NZ 's economy has grown ( aided by an earthquake in Christchurch ! ) by 25.9% ( an average of 3.7% p.a. ) -- 58% lower . Unemployment between 1999-2008 dropped by 48% across all regions . What 's wrong with that record ? <p> Tax has become a dirty word in New Zealand . In the 1960s and 70s tax rates were high but education and health services were almost free . Unfortunately , the decades since the advent of Rogernomics in 1984 , we have seen a steady push towards reducing tax on higher incomes , transferring the tax to consumption ( GST ) , and replacing free health care and education with a user-pays system that only benefits the more comfortable 25% of society . New Zealand has become steadily more unequal , despite the one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ I supported , to increase direct tax for high income earners by 6 cents in the dollar between 2000-2008 ) . <p> What needs to change is the attitude to paying tax . Tax is the glue that sticks society together . It is the means of transferring wealth from the haves to the have nots . This is vital for a healthy society . Without tax we are reduced to every person seizing what he or she can : succeed , and be one of the wealthy barricaded in gated communities ; fail , and sink into the abyss . <p> To have any hope of maintaining NZ Super and of restoring free health care , free education , meaningful ACC , and social welfare benefits that do not insult recipients , New Zealand needs to reverse its gradual flattening of the income tax rate and embrace the principle that graduated income tax is good . Income tax should be the deliberate tool to share wealth in society by requiring the wealthy to pay a much greater part of the social bill for health care , education , care of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disabled and the unemployed . That is the kind of New Zealand I would be proud to live in -- and let me add , capital gains tax ( CGT ) for tax equity issues alone , is surely a tax whose time has come . <p> We must also grow the economy , so investment in research and innovation must be increased in all areas of our economy : from agriculture , horticulture , forestry and fishing through to high-tech manufacturing . Dealing with climate change as a reality and not something to either deny or evade our responsibilities must also be a top priority . <p> If Labour wins the next election it will be leading a coalition government . MMP will almost always deliver such an outcome and it is not all that bad -- in fact it is often a victory for democracy . Gone are the days when 35% of the vote in a general election will see you elected as the government of New Zealand with a so-called ' mandate ' to govern . After all , it was the Alliance that led the regional @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fought for Paid Parental Leave and massive new investment in suicide prevention . We fought against any further sales of strategic assets , supported the Cullen Super Fund and , dare I say it , won a fierce battle to establish a new Kiwi-owned bank . You see -- politicians and politics can and should make a difference ! <p> To conclude then , and on a somewhat broader note , what of the future of the planet ? The most blatant international outrage is that the world -- which means the richest nations -- spends over $300 billion a year on armaments at a time when two thirds of the world 's population live in conditions of mounting misery . The palaeontologist Richard Leakey , when asked how he would describe a ' civilised society ' , answered ' a considerate society ' . <p> Fortunately there is increasing evidence -- in the use of terms such as ' interdependence ' , of the growing consciousness of the widespread abuse of human rights and in the debate on a new international order -- that the need to change the criteria @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ being widely recognised , however fearful and defensive that makes some of us . Those old attitudes which enabled people to ignore the wretchedness of others by burying it , covering it up with a kind of theological , biological or ideological mayonnaise have lost some of their magic because there is too much misery about . And , happily , equally old values of equity and compassion are once again being emphasized in public discussion . <p> People everywhere are being asked what they themselves can do to make the world more humane . Wisdom , in answering this question , lies in being able to see the future in terms of experience of the past . That may be the way to become good ancestors , and , it may also provide a good policy platform for not only winning elections but for staying in government for a long time . <p> Mayor Celia Wade-Brown says that by favouring the $150 million option over the cheaper , more basic alternative , the decision confirms the Council 's commitment to investing in the city to drive economic growth and global @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ both major parties have always spoken virtually as one ... so no surprise then , to hear that Labour has few problems with the government 's proposed extension of the powers of the intelligence agencies , which will legitimise the spying upon New Zealanders ... @@13875261 <h> New Zealand to ratify Paris agreement this year <p> The Government plans to ratify the Paris agreement on climate change by the end of the year , Climate Change Issues Minister Paula Bennett says . <p> The agreement was finalised last December and made available for signature in April this year . Ratification is the formal step that countries must take to be full participants and to ensure the deal takes effect . <p> " The Paris agreement is historic and changed the way the world thinks about climate change . Ratifying it early reinforces our commitment to this deal and our support for the global momentum to grow with lower emissions , " says Mrs Bennett . <p> Under the agreement , each nation is required to set an emissions reduction target . New Zealand committed to reducing its greenhouse gas emissions to 30 per cent below 2005 levels by 2030 . <p> " This is an ambitious target that wo n't be easy for New Zealand to achieve . All parts of society have a part to play in the transition to a lower emissions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , iwi and communities , " Mrs Bennett says . <p> " New Zealanders are already doing a lot of work to reduce our emissions , from being more energy conscious through to some of the exciting changes in agriculture . <p> " No matter how much New Zealand reduces its emissions , we will still feel the effects of a changing planet if other nations do not make significant changes . <p> " It is clear that we need to be better prepared to adapt to these changes , so I am establishing a group of technical experts to look at things like the effects on infrastructure and economic growth . " <p> Terms of reference and membership for the group will be released within weeks . <p> The Government plans to finalize ratification before the UN 's Framework Convention on Climate Change conference in November . <p> The Paris Agreement and a National Interest Analysis will today be tabled in the House of Representatives for consideration by a select committee . The Government will then sign and deliver the ratification document to the United Nations in New York . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have always spoken virtually as one ... so no surprise then , to hear that Labour has few problems with the government 's proposed extension of the powers of the intelligence agencies , which will legitimise the spying upon New Zealanders ... @@13877895 <h> Both sides in marine monument fight invoke Hawaiian culture <p> HONOLULU ( AP ) " As a Native Hawaiian living in modern Hawaii , one of the times Shad Kane truly felt connected to his ancestors was when he traveled to a remote , 140,000-square-mile area of the Pacific where islands , atolls , islets and coral reefs make up a federally protected marine monument . <p> There , at Midway Atoll , he plucked feathers from dead birds to make kahili " feathers mounted on a long wooden pole that was used to announce the presence of ancient Hawaiian chiefs and became a royal symbol during the Hawaiian monarchy . <p> " Going to Midway and being a part of gathering traditional birds in the manner they did is extremely spiritual , " the suburban Honolulu man said this month . " It becomes much more personal for me . " <p> He said seeing Midway strewn with remnants of fishing nets , buoys and hooks helped him realize the surrounding area needs protection , too . <p> This year , a group of Native Hawaiian @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Monument , while keeping the main Hawaiian islands outside the boundaries . The move would make the monument about 582,000 square miles , more than twice the size of Texas . <p> The White House is n't indicating when a decision will be made . Obama also has been asked to designate new national monuments in Utah , Arizona , Nevada , Maine and elsewhere . <p> The effort to expand the Pacific monument has supporters and opponents invoking Hawaiian culture to further their agendas . Some believe expansion of one of the world 's largest marine conservation areas will protect a sacred place , while others say making more waters off-limits will harm fishermen for a cause pushed by environmentalists with deep pockets . <p> A presidential proclamation established the monument in 2006 . Only scientific research and cultural activities are allowed , with commercial fishing and recreational activities such as diving banned . <p> Getting there requires taking a flight to Midway Atoll or a sailboat . We 're opposing what seems to be addressing a global problem , " he said of issues like climate change and overfishing that supporters point to . <p> He cited how Hawaiians utilized periods of kapu , or temporary restrictions in response to overharvesting . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said . <p> It 's difficult to estimate the financial effect that expansion would have on the $100 million per year longline industry , which supplies a large portion of the fresh tuna and other fish consumed in Hawaii , said Sean Martin , president of the Hawaii Longline Association . <p> He estimated about 2 million pounds of fish annually come from the proposed expansion area , where vessels string lines ranging from a mile to 50 miles long in the ocean to catch fish . <p> G. @@13897451 <h> These foods will become horrendously expensive in the next decade <p> Coffee is currently the world 's second most traded commodity , but that could all change quite soon . <p> When we think of climate change , we think about sea levels . <p> For those of us without beachfront property this makes it someone else 's problem . Our hillside homes will be fine . Maybe we 'll have some warmer weather . <p> But anthropogenic climate change will do much more than change our shorelines . One of the key areas almost everyone will feel the pain is in their shopping baskets . <p> Here are some of the foods and drinks scientists expect to be the worst hit . <p> MAARTEN HOLL/FAIRFAX NZ <p> Most of the world 's chocolate comes from west Africa . <p> Chocolate <p> Over half of the world 's chocolate supply comes from Ghana and the Ivory Coast in west Africa , an area a 2011 study said would look vastly different by 2050 . Temperatures will rise while water supplies will fall , resulting in cocoa trees @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ growing cocoa will shrink considerably , and growers are likely to refocus from volume to quality - so we should see far less chocolate , but it will probably be tastier . <p> The study expects chocolate supplies to significant decline by 2030 , but effects could be felt even sooner . <p> Indeed , thanks to rising demand and tree disease we are already in a chocolate deficit , eating more of it than we can currently produce . Two of the world 's largest chocolate makers expect that deficit to reach 1 million tonnes by just 2020 . <p> What does more acid mean ? Well for a start : probably less shellfish , as their calcified shells grow less and less sturdy . Other fish are also expected to adapt poorly , swimming to different areas where there are more predators thank to warming , or simply collapsing as a species . Oof . <p> Wine <p> Wine grape growing is extremely climate-sensitive , meaning small changes in temperatures and rainfall can vastly affect the taste and quantity of wine vineyards are able to produce . <p> Vast @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2013 study suggests up to 73 percent of Australia 's wine growing area and 70 percent of California 's will be unsuitable by 2050 . <p> But there is some good news : New Zealand 's going to become a better place to grow wine , with larger and larger areas suitable for vineyards . And as the global wine shortage from those other areas kicks in , we can expect to sell that extra wine for a pretty good price . <p> Reuters <p> Coffee <p> Coffee is the world 's favourite drink . It 's the second most traded commodity ( after oil . ) . But that all could change in not too long . <p> How hurt ? One model showed it losing 99.7 percent of its suitable locations by 2080 , while the best case saw it losing 65 percent . <p> It gets worse ! That 's just looking at Arabica in a vacuum , but plants do n't grow in vacuums . Bees have been dying in record rates and nobody knows quite why , although pesticides are likely a factor . Furthermore , this study shows bees are extremely bad at adapting to new conditions , so we can expect things to get worse . <p> But we do n't eat bees , right ? <p> Right . But their pollen is important in the production of a huge amount food crops : from apples to kiwifruit to avocados to beans to cabbage . <p> These effects are likely to be less immediate than the ones that hit more exposed crops like chocolate and coffee . But unless things change in a drastic way , they 're sure to come eventually .

@@13910571 <h> U.S. village in Alaska votes to relocate due to climate change <p> The tiny island north of the Bering Strait , pictured , is losing up to three metres of shoreline each year . <p> A Native American village in the U.S. state of Alaska has voted to relocate its entire population of some 600 people due to the threat of rising seas , officials said on Friday . <p> Shishmaref , located on a tiny island north of the Bering Strait that separates the United States and Russia , is losing up to 10 feet ( three metres ) of shoreline each year , according to research by the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers and Alabama-based Auburn University . <p> Shishmaref is one of dozens of indigenous villages in Alaska that face growing threats of flooding and erosion due to global warming , according to a report by the U.S. Government Accountability Office . <p> Shishmaref residents , who are members of the Inupiat tribe , voted 89 to 78 to relocate , said Donna Barr , secretary of the Shishmaref Council . Voting took place @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ yet been formally certified and a handful of absentee ballots remain unopened . <p> This is not the first time Shishmaref residents have made such a decision . They voted in 2002 to relocate but that effort stalled . <p> A few other U.S. communities in Alaska , Washington and Louisiana have decided recently to relocate as well due to climate change and shoreline erosion , according to the Washington , D.C.-based Arctic Institute . <p> Several others are likely weighing options to move , said Christina DeConcini , a spokeswoman for the Washington , D.C.-based World Resources Institute . <p> " It 's not going to be the last time that the United States has to deal with communities severely threatened by climate change and impacts and whether or not they can stay there , " she said . <p> " There are people here who have been here for generations and do n't want to leave at all , " he told the Thomson Reuters Foundation . <p> The cost of relocation has been estimated at some US$180 million , and authorities are seeking state and federal funding , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to be decided , the clerk said . Its economy is based largely on fishing and hunting . <p> Scientists attribute coastal erosion in Shishmaref to global warming that has thawed sea ice that once shielded the island from storm surges . New Zealand is mainly made up of two small mountainous islands surrounded by ocean and smack bang in the roaring forties - in other words , anything can happen and we can sometimes buck the global weather trend in some areas . @@13928570 <h> Heading into a future of extremes <p> With fires , floods and rising seas forcing a village to move , Chris Mooney asks if events this week are a sign of things to come . <p> Fires in California this week surged across more than 120 square kilometres and forced 82,000 people to flee their homes . Picture / AP <p> In San Bernardino County , California , 82,000 people were ordered to leave their homes on Thursday as an explosive wildfire " hit with an intensity that we had n't seen before " , as one fire official said , and surged across more than 120 square kilometres . <p> It followed dramatic flooding in Louisiana that the Red Cross termed the " worst natural disaster " in the United States since Hurricane Sandy four years ago . " <p> As is repeated ad nauseam , attributing any individual disaster to climate change remains tricky , especially in real time . Floods happen regularly in even a normal climate , and fires can start from human carelessness or even arson . Moreover , their damage is made worse by people living ever closer to what is called the " wildland urban interface " , which puts them in the way of fires . Climate change did n't cause sprawl . <p> While such climatic factors as heat , drought and earlier seasonal snowmelt surely exacerbate matters ( for fires ) , it 's hard to say that they pulled the trigger . " However , the question is whether the frequency , spatial area , intensity and duration of such events is increasing under climate change . " <p> He continued : " Evidence suggests that they are indeed increasing along many of these dimensions , and that the number of people displaced by these events is growing in parallel . <p> " There are ongoing efforts to attribute specific events to climate change - and that work needs to continue - but climate researchers have documented that the risk of such events would be expected to increase under a changing climate . @@13928582 <p> Alex does n't remember the details . The moment she became he . The moment the daughter became a son , the sister became a brother . <p> In her head that moment had happened months , even years earlier . As she watched her girlfriends grow out of their tomboy phases , wanting to call out " Wait for me " . As she realised that , while her friends were attracted to guys , she was attracted to being a guy . As she broke down , while trying on her older sister 's dress on holiday in Germany , realising the body she saw in that mirror was a prison she needed to escape . <p> That was the moment she came out to herself , but about six months went by before she told her trusted Onslow College music teacher . Doctors say family acceptance remains the biggest issue for transgender young people . <p> The Kers are not your average Kiwi family . Sociology texts litter their Karori lounge . The kids grew up without television . Mum , Christiane , is German and a primary school teacher . Dad Alastair - a university lecturer - bikes home in the rain , partly to avoid contributing to climate change . But Alex was still terrified of their reaction . <p> A HAPPY , NORMAL CHILDHOOD <p> Growing up , Alex was just one of the girls . <p> A wilful character , she decided at about age 9 to ditch piano lessons and teach herself . When she wanted a dog , she delivered a Powerpoint presentation anticipating her parents ' probable objections . <p> She loved anything to do with animals . She and her Dad would act out the animal of the week . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ She was n't much into skirts or dresses and wore her hair long but plain . But they never had gender-specific toys and she was n't excessively tomboyish . It was just a happy , normal childhood , Christiane says . <p> It 's not unusual for transgender young people to only develop body issues during puberty , but the lens of hindsight illuminates possible clues . Mortified at being a flouncy fairy in A Midsummer Night 's Dream , 11-year-old Alex negotiated to be a Pacific sprite , in baggy Hawaiian shirt and jeans . <p> " I was sitting over there , " Alastair says , pointing to the living room sofa . New Zealand faces a wave of treatment demand from young people who feel trapped in the wrong body , and we 're not equipped to deal with it , experts say . <p> Reliable figures are scarce . The Youth 2012 survey found 1.2 per cent of teens identified as transgender . <p> Use of puberty-delaying drugs has spiked , suggesting teens are seeking help earlier . New figures show the number of 10 to 14-year-olds taking the drugs has risen from 27 in 2005 to 95 in 2015 . <p> Wellington counsellor Mani Mitchell says growing social acceptance of transgender young people has spawned a surge similar to that seen in the gay and lesbian communities in the 50s and 60s . <p> " We basically have a tsunami and we 're not ready . We do n't have enough people trained . It is n't funded adequately . " <p> Adolescent physician at Auckland 's Centre for Youth Health , Rachel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ started working with transgender young people in 2008 . Now she works with about 100 , mostly aged 12 to 24 . <p> Access to puberty blockers has revolutionised trans treatment options for young people , she says . Hormone treatment - regular injections of hormones from the desired gender - is only available from 16 and can have irreversible effects , such as infertility . So puberty blockers buy time , while preventing the body transformations transgender adults spend so much time and money trying to reverse . If the teen changes their mind - which they occasionally do - they can stop the blockers and normal ( albeit delayed ) puberty will progress . <p> The final puzzle piece - gender reassignment surgery - is a luxury afforded only the wealthy . With only four operations funded every two years , transpeople are being told they must wait 30 or 40 years to qualify . Since the retirement of New Zealand 's only gender reassignment surgeon , Peter Walker , in 2014 , there have been no publicly-funded reassignment operations . <p> But the biggest issue for trans young @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " If you 're not accepted by your family , it 's so much harder to be who you were meant to be . Contrary to popular understanding , being transgender is not a lifestyle choice , Alex says . <p> The best evidence of that is the chest compression vest , known as a binder , that transmen ( females transitioning to male ) wear to hide their female breasts . To wear it for more than 8-10 hours is a health hazard . <p> " You can feel it completely constricting everything around your chest , but it 's the sacrifice you make because it 's better than having to deal with a female chest . At the end of the day , when you take it off , you want to feel like it 's relieving - oh @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ feel imprisoned , because you still have that body . Damned if you do , damned if you do n't . " <p> YOU 'VE LOST A DAUGHTER , BUT GAINED A SON <p> Alex 's parents knew opposing his transition would help no-one . Who knows what would have happened had they said " No , we wo n't allow it , come back tomorrow " , Christiane says . They just wanted to listen and be there for him and that 's their advice to other parents . <p> That 's not to say it was easy . Like many parents of transgender young people , Christiane felt grief . She had lost her third daughter . She asked herself - but girls can do anything , be a cowboy or a mechanic , so why such a radical change ? But then she realised Alex was still Alex , she just had a different gender . <p> Coming to terms with it was one thing . Telling others was another . Christiane told only one teaching colleague . <p> " I was n't the first one to stand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to tell you about my son ' ... At the very beginning I found it very difficult . I could hardly speak of it before I started to cry . Just now , in the last half year , I say matter-of-factly ' We have two girls and one boy ' . " <p> There was a funny call to her sister in Germany . " I need to tell you something about Alex " . " Ja , we know " . They had seen Alex 's Facebook post but waited for Christiane to mention it . <p> Alastair tried to remember that whatever he was feeling came second to Alex 's troubles . He did n't feel a loss , because " the core of who Alex was transcends gender " . But people 's glib comments " Oh , you 've lost a daughter but gained a son " did n't exactly represent the whole picture . However , over the course of about a year , he came to see Alex as a son . <p> " Now I feel that is true - we do relate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ known - I would have taught Alex how to repair a bike puncture sooner . I tried with my daughters , but they did n't show much interest . " <p> Also by Alex 's side was Maike Martini - his friend since German playgroup as two-year-olds . She supported him through school , through the rollercoaster of his transition and the pair still catch up about once a week , to play scrabble , eat , sing and play guitar . Her attitude reflects the massive strides in awareness of the next generation . <p> " Everyone changes over time anyway , everyone is developing . I was changing , my opinions changed , I got new interests . It 's just another kind of change . He did n't change his personality in any drastic ways . It was n't an overnight thing . It was the whole process , and we 're both still changing now . " <p> But none of them could have anticipated the crisis to come . <p> SPEAKING OUT <p> Monday night at 128 Abel Smith St - the activist house now used @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bike repair workshop and swap-a-tunity shop leftovers and settle into the couches in front of the open fire . <p> " My body , my choice " , reads a banner on the wall . <p> Since he came out , Alex has campaigned to improve transgender awareness . Now , it 's this InsideOUT support group for gender diverse people , with whom he organised a national Day of Silence for victims of social prejudice . <p> There 's a prayer meeting vibe - they start by talking about their day . Alex spent his talking to Wellington Girls ' pupils with fellow InsideOUT member Tabby . They talked about plans for skirt-short hybrids to make the uniform more inclusive for trans-students , but Tabby pointed out making shorts look feminine kind of defeats the point . <p> Alex occasionally wonders where he 'd be if he 'd attended Wellington Girls ' and been forced into a skirt on top of everything else . " Would I be alive ? I know that sounds harsh , but I sometimes think about that . " <p> Next is a debrief about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and support in Wellington are comprehensive , not everywhere is so lucky . <p> A New Zealand Medical Journal paper in May found " significant barriers to care in many regions " . Treatment templates are inconsistent . <p> Auckland sexual health doctor Rick Franklin says while progress has been incredibly slow , the four northern health boards at least have a plan to standardise and improve transgender services . <p> The biggest issue , he says , is access for those living outside the main centres , who would struggle to find a doctor who even understands what they 're experiencing . <p> Tabby has awful news to share about Whanganui . Someone in the gender diverse community there has committed suicide . <p> " It 's quite horrible and a real shock to hear that a young person died from that community . But it also reinforces how important it is in rural communities to be talking about this stuff . " <p> Alex Ker <p> Alex Ker chronicles the changes to his female body during a year of testosterone treatment . <p> CRISIS AND CONFIDENCE <p> Transgender people @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ anxiety and depression . Untreated , they have suicide rates as high as 20 per cent . It 's impossible to unpick cause and effect , but it 's hardly surprising when you factor in bullying , family rejection , body dysphoria and limited treatment access . <p> In many ways , Alex has had the best experience possible , with incredible support from his parents , friends and school . But he was not immune . <p> When he finally started two-weekly testosterone injections the effect was transformational . He shot video to plot the gradual deepening of his voice and took photos to chronicle the slow morphing of his round chops to a more angular , masculine shape . <p> Two years on , Alex 's voice is undeniably blokey but he 's still hoping for more stubble . It 's ruined his singing voice , which is a drag as that 's something he loves . <p> But there were more significant side effects - it 's like turning into a pubescent boy , with all the accompanying mood swings . <p> Today is the day before his @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ But he - and his family - have learnt to manage the two-week cycle . <p> Alex with sister Helen , who has supported him through his transition . <p> The side effect no-one anticipated was Alex 's eating disorder . When he saw his fat redistributing , he seized on the chance to control his curves . <p> " It pushed me to think if I do n't have any fat on my body I have as close to a male body as I can get ... It stuffs your brain up . You think you 're in control and you 're actually out of control . " <p> It took Maike and his older sister Helen to make him get help , which culminated in Alex spending months in hospital and at the eating disorder service last year . <p> Alastair 's eyes leak silent tears at the memory . It was a triple whammy - dysphoria , moodiness and then the eating disorder . <p> " When the eating disorder was at its most acute , we were really in crisis management mode " <p> Alex has shown @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ educate others , Alastair says . " But the simple mundane task of living from day to day is in some ways harder than that and it 's more of a marathon . It requires endurance and resilience . " <p> What helped pull Alex out was the prospect of " top surgery " - an operation to remove his female breasts so he would no longer have to bind . He grins just saying the words . <p> " I would literally go to bed every night and dream about getting top surgery and wake and feel like s ... because I would look down and be like ' Oh , great ' . It was literally a dream come true when it happened . " <p> There 's no public funding . Alex had planned to work to save $15,000 , but when it became clear just how serious his condition was , his parents helped pay . Initially , it seemed Thailand was the only option , but they eventually found a willing local surgeon . <p> That was last November . Christiane took a photo the moment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ beaming . <p> Ross Giblin <p> Alex Ker , 19 is transgender and is transitioning from female to male . He has now had chest surgery to become flat chested and has been taking testosterone for two years . <p> Since Alex 's surgery it has been a slow grind back to health . There are still roadblocks . Alex has changed his New Zealand passport gender ( you can use X but he 's opted for male ) . But to change his German passport , he has to change his gender on his birth certificate , which requires going to court . <p> Like many trans people , Alex has no interest in gender reassignment or " bottom surgery " . Although he looks and talks like a bloke , he now feels " 97 per cent male " . If a form has a male and female box , he 'll tick male . If there 's an ' other ' option , he 'll tick that . <p> " I used to identify as a straight female . Now I identify as an asexual transgender guy ... @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ I want to acknowledge the fact I 'm not just a guy , but a trans guy and there 's something positive and cool about that , rather than it being a hindrance to who I am . " <p> But finally , the person in the mirror is the same person he sees in his head . <p> " I see a silhouette that is Alex , that defines who I am . I do n't necessarily see a masculine body , or a feminine body , which I think is really liberating , because I 've been able to self-determine the shape of my body , how I come across physically to the world . But I have much more respect for the thing in the mirror as well . I feel like I can finally own it and embrace it and celebrate it . " 
@@13931160 <p> New Zealand 's restaurants and pubs have changed a great deal in the last 10 years , and in the next decade they 're likely to change even more . Rose Hoare speaks to our greatest bar and restaurant identities to get a sense of the forces that are transforming the hospitality industry . <p> a special longform feature brought you by The Spinoff and DB Breweries <p> Rose Hoare speaks to chefs , architects , critics and hospitality identities to evaluate the state of the art in eating and drinking -- without once using the word ' foodie ' . <h> Even trends are getting trendier <p> Let 's address the elephant in the room right away : kale has peaked . Although still popular with dairy cows as a winter feed option , it has been on the slide in New Zealand since January last year , according to Google Trends . And if you think I 'm going to be talking about cronuts , you can forget it . They flatlined shortly after appearing in 2013 , and I feel embarrassed for you that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that what we eat and drink can be considered so culturally significant that it competes for space alongside Blac Chyna and national elections in news headlines but , apparently , such is the mystique of doughnuts . <p> Blame the appeal of charismatic TV chefs like Jamie Oliver ( who people love because he does n't measure anything ) and Nigella Lawson ( sexy ) and Gordon Ramsey ( good swearing ) . Blame the popularity of cooking shows like Masterchef and My Kitchen Rules and Chef 's Table , which has just been renewed for three more seasons . <p> Blame Instagram , with its ability to popularise things like kale salads and rainbow lattes , which look much better than they taste . Blame Ren ? Redzepi , a very thoughtful and serious innovator , whose careful experiments in reinventing Nordic cuisine at his Copenhagen restaurant Noma have spawned so many imitators . <p> For whatever reason , it seems that in the last 15 years or so , we have come to care about food and drink more than ever . " A lot more people are eating @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ McVinnie . " People are choosing to spend their money on restaurants , whereas they did n't use to . Food is taken a lot more seriously than it used to be . <p> " I watched two old guys on a train in Italy once , discussing how you make spaghetti carbonara for two hours . I spoke to a woman about ricotta for two and a half hours . We do n't take food quite that seriously or have that very special relationship with food that old cultures have , but I think we 're getting towards it . " <p> These days , bars and restaurants , their customers , and the social media that brings them together , all thrive on novelty . Going out has become a trend-hunt , which has driven -- or forced -- more innovation . <p> John Hellebrekers , who as managing director of Barworks oversees 19 Auckland venues , says people decide where to go out based on what 's trending . " You need to keep introducing new flavours that people are craving , " he says . " Social @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are n't as loyal . Instead of going down to your local on Saturday night , people are wanting something they have n't experienced before . " <p> In Auckland , new bars and restaurants open every day . There are already around 9,000 food premises , and for the last four years , Auckland Council has been approving new licences at a rate of around 700-800 a year , making Auckland a tough place to compete -- or even find staff . <p> " There 's not been the growth of people working in hospitality , relative to the growth of the industry , " Hellebrekers warns . " If we did n't have immigrants coming in to fill these positions , we 'd be in dire straits , really . There 's pressure on HR , there 's pressure on wages to increase , and it 's not going to get any better because of the growth of tourism . " <p> Dining precincts like Ponsonby Central and CityWorks Depot in Auckland , Eat Streat in Rotorua , or Leed Street in Wellington are having some success by offering @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , The Hip Group and The Nourish Group can similarly hedge their bets , spreading risk and discovering operational efficiencies and economies of scale . <p> To survive , bar and restaurant owners need to figure out how to move with the times . " Due to fit-out costs and increasing rents and labour costs , I think you 'll see more smaller venues being built , " Hellebrekers predicts . " You might open a venue with a certain style , but you 'll know in the back of your mind that in three years ' time it 's going to be a different concept . " <p> Although punishing for owners , the intensified interest and competition has meant that the overall quality of our restaurants has improved , and even your average mid-range pub will now have chef-y flourishes on the menu . <p> In 2000 , if you 'd walked into one of the joints crammed in on Auckland 's waterfront , you 'd probably have had a choice of three or four beers on tap and an overpriced menu of basic greasies . If you did @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Today , if you wandered into The Crew Club , a vast gastro-pub near the Maritime Museum , you could have a kedgeree with ras el hanout , pickled egg , mussels and golden raisins for your brunch ( the fact that you could have brunch at all is pretty revolutionary ) , or a paleo chia custard thing if you 're health conscious , or interesting dishes like Hawaiian poke . There are five low-strength beers to choose from -- that 's just the low-strength beers -- and you know the food will be good because the chef just came from Clooney . <p> Increasingly , the distinction between high-end and cheap-and-cheerful is blurring . In fact , everything 's blurring ! Where restaurants used to offer food service , caf ? service and bar service in order to reach as many people as possible , these days , restaurants , cafes and bars are all narrowing what they offer . <p> " Caf ? s have gone in their own direction , extracting this and cold drip that , " explains Renee Coulter , who co-owns popular @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " There are places that have stripped it right back to where you just get your shot of coffee at the bar , through to the full brunch with table service thing . Bars have done the same . Each area is getting fine-tuned and everyone 's defining what they do . " <p> " And within restaurants , everyone 's defining what they do . Apero is a good example : people are like , what are you ? Why do n't you be a restaurant with more choices ? They 're like : we wan na be a bar that does amazing food . " <p> Perhaps a sign of how fluid everything 's getting : Coco 's Cantina will turn half of its space into a bar with no table service , while half will continue to be semi-fancy dining . <p> Increasingly , New Zealanders are just looking for more options . They want somewhere they can go out for a few drinks , eat some nice food ( without killing the vibe ) , and to have the option to go somewhere else afterwards . They @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ point . They 're embracing beer as a food match in a way which was once the exclusive preserve of wine . <p> Places that are narrow in concept or single-proposition ( meaning they specialise in one type of thing ) and low-cost ( meaning that thing is cheap to make ) are reaping the profits , while the closure of Hammerheads and Kermadec in Auckland suggests that serving expensive seafood in a huge waterfront space -- something that used to represent the pinnacle of New Zealand hospitality -- now seems like a great way to lose money . Tellingly , at SkyCity 's newest restaurant , the signature dish is a $13.50 cheeseburger . <p> In the kitchen at Cassia , Auckland . Beer drinking : will craft go mainstream ? <p> Craft is exploding in an otherwise low-growth beer market . But beer is experiencing a 40 year trend of very gradual decline . Will craft and other growth areas -- including premium , low-alcohol and cider -- help reverse that phenomenon ? <p> 7 . Interiors <p> How open kitchens make your food taste better , and why the niching of the industry might mean restaurant and bar fit-outs will get crazier. <h> 1 . Everyone wants to be healthier ( by day ) ... <p> " Clean eating " -- the practice of favouring whole foods , the rawer the better , and avoiding gluten , sugar , dairy and meat -- has been accused of being an eating disorder , deemed anti-feminist , and lampooned as unobtainable . But it 's rooted in a widespread and thoroughgoing concern about nutrition that 's trickling up , down and sideways into every level of food service , so even if you think it 's a bunch of bollocks , history will prove you wrong @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , wheat and dairy-free products , regardless of whether they have food intolerances , because they think they 're healthier . Traceability , sustainability and efforts to minimise waste are also increasingly on customers ' minds , and are therefore increasingly a selling-point . <p> Around the world , businesses are scrambling to cleanse their menus of undesirable ingredients , or offer healthy alternatives . <p> This context makes it easier to understand the success of Little Bird Organics , which launched in 2010 with a range of organic macaroons , granola and crackers , and has since expanded to involve three Auckland caf ? s serving dishes free from dairy , gluten , cane sugar and soy . They make their own nut milks , nut cheeses and coconut yoghurt from scratch , and since opening , they 've been fully cranking . <p> Founder Megan May was as surprised as anyone that her first " unbakery " was an instant hit . " It was an odd combination of people who had been waiting for something like this for a really long time -- people like me -- and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , you 'd get all the fashion people , " she says . <p> Although she was initially careful to make everything taste traditionally yummy , May has noticed that her core customers now crave the grassy , sour , bitter flavours of things like ginger , turmeric , and lemon . <p> " Whether it 's through us , or regular cafes that are serving green smoothies , people are getting a taste for it . I was totally addicted to sugar as a kid , but actually this food can be really addictive as well . It makes you feel a bit cleaner . It 's like having a shower , almost . " <p> Next , May plans to introduce more herbs and mushrooms from Chinese medicine , which she says , " can come with some interesting flavour that is a little bit harder to jazz up " . <p> A Bearded Clam chilli cheeseburger , which aims to resemble a McDonald 's cheeseburger , but in a boutique way . Photo supplied <h> 2 . ... And unhealthy by night <p> What 's paradoxical about the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ elevated ' street food . We 're eating chia granola and drinking charcoal smoothies by day , then gorging on gourmet burgers and fried chicken after dark . <p> Burgers , in particular , have gone from being perceived as " what fat people eat " , as food writer AA Gill puts it , to being cool . Maybe not as cool as drugs , but just as much a part of a big night out for hipsters in London , Paris and New York , where the trend originated . <p> In 2000 , decorated New York chef Danny Meyer opened a concession stand in Madison Square Park called Shake Shack , serving basic but good-quality burgers made with 100% natural Angus beef patties . By the time it listed on the NY Stock Exchange last year , Shake Shack was valued at US$1.6 billion and it 's just one player in a global market that 's still growing . <p> In Auckland , Ryan Kneebone and Adam Crickett cook burgers sporadically at pop-up events as The Bearded Clam . Their patties are high-grade beef cooked medium-rare , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they use pre-sliced Mainland catering cheese , Heinz ketchup , French 's mustard and Best Foods mayo , because their intention is to approximate a McDonald 's cheeseburger , " in a controlled , boutique sort of way " . <p> But the fact that there 's now four other restaurants doing similarly American-styled burgers in the central city alone suggests consumers are catching on . For those who do n't have the money or the will to sit in a restaurant for hours spending big bucks on a three-course meal , gourmet burgers are cheap and quick , and , crucially , pair very well with beer . <p> They 're also both accessible and exclusive : there 's nothing intimidating about a cheeseburger , but if you add recherche ingredients like kimchi , they become something special . <p> Burgers are at the sweet spot where convenience , price and novelty -- but not too much novelty -- meet . They 're hipster comfort food that , like fried chicken , gourmet hotdogs and other elevated street foods , taste that much better once you 've had a couple @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ team at Cassia , left , and one of his elegant desserts , right . Photos : Rebecca Zephyr Thomas <h> 3 . Please , let 's stop saying ' modern ethnic ' <p> Although New Zealand 's population is still three-quarters European , Auckland is a different story . A quarter of the city now identifies as Asian , with Indian , Chinese and Filipino the most common ethnic groups . Thanks to these and other migrant populations , a lot of New Zealanders are now at ease eating and cooking lots of different cuisines , and exploring how their flavours can be reworked . <p> Parallel with this exploration is a growing awareness that a chef from one ethnic background might be interested in cooking the cuisine of another ethnicity ( an Indian chef might be good at cooking French food , for instance ) . And , just as freaky , a chef from a particular ethnic background ( let 's say Portuguese ) might tinker with his or her own parent cuisine , creating dishes that are not , strictly speaking , " authentic " or " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Portuguese " , to reflect the departure from tradition . ( Or , if it 's a non-European cuisine being reworked , the cringey designation " modern ethnic " apparently covers all . ) <p> Although French cooking techniques are still highly valued as a building block for experimentation , old cultural hierarchies have toppled . The clearest proof is that some of the country 's best formal restaurants serve careful or playful interpretations of cuisines that used to be relegated to the ' cheap and cheerful ' category : Thai , Indian , Japanese , Vietnamese , Chinese , Korean and Middle Eastern . <p> Ten years ago , diners might have blanched at paying more than $10 or $12 for Indian food , and may have been confused to find an Indian restaurant that does n't do butter chicken or rogan josh . This year , Metro magazine 's Restaurant of the Year is a fine dining Indian restaurant that does paneer and goat 's cheese fritters ( $30 ) , a duck and chicken ' stirfry ' with kadhai spices ( $38 ) , really elegant , finely-wrought @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the vibe ' buzzy ' . <p> But before opening Cassia in 2014 , chef Sid Sahrawat had probably cooked Italian more than any other cuisine . As a youngster , he did a hotel management course in Chennai , then an apprenticeship in Oman , where he cooked in a busy hotel restaurant called Tuscany . He came to New Zealand in 2000 , aged 20 , and worked at Toto and Non Solo Pizza for three years . <p> He and his wife Chand dreamed of opening a restaurant like Cassia . Instead , he opened his degustation restaurant , Sidart , building it into something that 's earned three hats in Cuisine 's Good Food Guide and been voted Auckland 's #1 restaurant on Trip Advisor . <p> At Cassia , he 's showcasing the dimensions of flavour and the variation that make Indian food so interesting , using classic French cooking techniques , and New Zealand produce like pumpkin , kale and kumara . <p> " If I 'd opened Cassia before Sidart , it might not have been as big a success as it is now @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ says . " We 've had a lot of people come here because they trust what we 've done in the past . There 's a great sense of positivity in Auckland diners at the moment . People are very accepting of everything going on here . Half of our diners let the front of house pick for them . " <h> 4 . Food your grandmother would recognise <p> A couple of years ago , a decorated food writer skewered the trend for highly innovative , highly finicky food , painstakingly compiled with tweezers by young men seeking to push culinary boundaries , in the footsteps of Rene Redzepi . <p> " This is the first food development in America that exists not because customers are eager for it but because chefs insist on doing it , " sniped food writer Alan Richman , labelling the style ' New Nordic Dude Food ' and " Egotarian " cuisine . <p> This struck a chord with chef David Bach . He 'd been cooking at places like Lyles in London ( reviewed as " disgustingly cool " by The Telegraph ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' ' neo-bistro ' trend ) . When Bach returned home this year , he wanted to work at Siostra in Auckland 's Grey Lynn , run by Esther Lamb ( front of house ) and her sister Beki Lamb ( head chef ) . <p> Siostra is a pretty straightforward neighbourhood restaurant , but often there are small but appreciable flourishes of nostalgia on its menu . A popular entree there is devils on horseback -- tea-soaked prunes wrapped in bacon -- which dates back to Victorian times and , I can attest , was still a smash-hit party snack in Northcote in the 1980s . <p> Other retro gestures have included a gribiche sauce ( you 'll find a recipe in Elizabeth David 's 1960 classic , French Provincial Cooking ) , scallops served on the half-shell , slow-cooked classics like veal ossobuco , and old-school ingredients like brussels sprouts and crab meat . <p> The devils were inspired by April Bloomfield , a British expat and traditionalist who cooks Michelin-starred gastro-pub fare at The Spotted Pig in New York , and Esther says they 've become an unalterable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ amused and pleased , " she says . " They 're quite affectionate about that dish . " <p> With arcane and tricky dishes on the rise amongst Serious Young Chefs , perhaps now more than ever there 's an increasing appetite for food that does n't exhaust the intellect . <p> Although you 'd think there 'd be less glory for a chef who refrains from putting their own ' modern twist ' on classic dishes , serious reviewers have been unstinting in their praises of new restaurants cooking retro food . <p> In New York earlier this year , two chefs who 've won Michelin stars for their Thai food opened Mr Donahue 's , where you can eat roast beef with onion rings and ranch dressing , rotisserie chicken , or shrimp cocktail at a doily-lined table , on plates " happily crammed with food , as if composed by someone who cares less about presentation and more about how much you 're getting to eat . " <p> " We 're not supposed to want to eat this way anymore , " the New York Times confided @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one of " gratitude " . <p> The same month in Sydney , a big hospitality group opened Restaurant Hubert , a brand new venture with food and d ? cor that mimics a post-war French bistro , serving chicken fricassee in tarragon gravy , oeufs en gel ? e , and creme caramel , which you eat surrounded by wood panelling and red velvet drapes . The only thing that feels genuinely new about Hubert is how faithfully it follows tradition . The Good Food Guide , which gives out the chef 's hats that are Australia 's answer to Michelin stars , actually went all caps for its review : " I LOVE THIS PLACE " . <p> " People respond incredibly positively to that Michael Pollan thing of food your great-grandmother would recognise , " Esther says . " It 's not about trying to make us look good , it 's trying to deliver something to the customer that makes them feel restored and nourished and welcome . I think those old standards are appealing to people for that reason . " <p> Chef Michael Meredith ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) makes a salad of chicken thighs and hearts , broccoli and edamame in a miso wasabi ginger dressing at Laffare Melrose in Auckland . For every salad sold , a hungry kid gets a lunch via his charity . Photos : Rebecca Zephyr Thomas <h> 5 . Behind the smiles : service with integrity <p> One feature of working in the hospitality industry is being treated to an endless parade of hedonism on the restaurant floor , seeing a constant flow of perfectly good food wasted in the kitchen , while knowing that 29% of New Zealand kids are growing up in poverty , homelessness is on the rise and climate change is going to do us all in if we do n't get it together soon . <p> Happily , some of the most successful people in hospitality in New Zealand are coming up with ways to offset the industry 's innate extravagance . This , more than anything else , is the most significant trend to have emerged in service lately . <p> It was only a year ago that one of New Zealand 's greatest chefs , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lunch , a scheme whereby a lunch bought means another lunch is donated to a hungry schoolkid . Today , Eat My Lunch makes close to 1,300 lunches a day for Auckland schools . ( Meredith was there at 5.30am making sandwiches the morning after his restaurant won Metro 's Fine Dining Restaurant of the Year award ) . In June , a crowd-funded social bonds scheme was launched to raise money for the next phase , which will involve launching operations in Wellington . <p> At his super-fancy restaurant , Meredith also supports charities for two-month stretches , raising tens of thousands of dollars by offering a weekly 4-course degustation menu for a donation . <p> Meredith sees this flow of support from a fine dining restaurant to the wider community as entirely natural . " People want to help , " he says . " Giving from our hearts is a big thing for most people . That 's how it works : Eat My Lunch works because people want to do something . " <p> At Coco 's Cantina , initiatives that serve the wider community are baked @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cooked meal to the NZ Prostitutes Collective nearby , and unsold food goes to the City Mission . <p> " There are people who do way more than us but we 're a busy restaurant , so we have just incorporated systems into our own , " Renee says . " Instead of making one staff dinner on Friday , we make two . " <p> " Running a business like that is more time-consuming . Boxing up all the stuff to take down to the Mission is an added cost that people do n't put into their business model , but we do , because we hate waste and we hate inequality , " Damaris says . " I 'm amazed at people who just look after their own lives . What a fucking luxury -- to just go to work , get paid and look after you and your family . To me , that 's unbelievable luxury . " <p> That hatred of inequality can also be seen in how Coco 's staff relate to customers . Extremely popular with a young , lively , occasionally messy crowd @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a style of service has evolved that 's more casual , more genuine , and more empowered , with a non-traditional assumption of equality between staff and customers . <p> In the ' 90s , when both sisters first began waitressing , Damaris explains , the style of service was " a lot more pretentious , but not as knowledgeable or sophisticated " . <p> " Back in the day , if you were serving a table , you sort of had to laugh at their shit jokes , you had to go along with their conversation , you had to ignore racist comments . You even had to ignore hands on the small of your back or lower down , " Damaris says . <p> " Here , if you wan na come out for dinner and get into conversation with our waiters , be prepared to hear their opinion . They 're not gon na give you something regurgitated . They 're definitely not gon na have one hand behind their back . And if you are racist , you have to go . " <p> " The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ apply here , " Renee says . " On every situation , it 's equal . Something 's happened ? Let 's fix it . I do n't care who is the problem . I 'll just fix the problem , and we 'll split the mistake , or , if we completely dropped the ball , I will own it . " <p> This ethos might sound alienating for customers , but Coco 's has attracted a like-minded and loyal clientele , which just goes to show that New Zealanders are getting better at accepting a restaurant on its own terms , whatever they might be . <p> Staff are supported to be themselves , but they 're also trained in the kind of imaginative empathy that keeps them grounded . <p> " You have to drill this into staff , and remind them : how would I be serving my Poppa , who ca n't hear ? " Renee says . " Would I turn the music down for him ? How would I be serving my parents who are a bit awkward and do n't go out that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's the loveliest man in the world -- so generous , so honest , so hard working -- but he can come across gruff , and he might not say please or thank you . When something becomes confusing , my Mum might get a little bit defensive and that 'll come across as rude . You have to train staff to recognise that hey , no one is instantly rude . You have to give them leeway . " <p> This is something that 's hard to judge , unless you are that rare thing in hospitality : a highly experienced waiter . It 's often assumed that New Zealand needs more long-term hospitality staff to establish a more professional service culture . Renee doubts that 's attainable or even desirable . <p> " Everyone has a timeframe where they can be in hospo and then you get burnt out . It 's hard being a waitress 10 years later , 20 years later , polishing cutlery at two o'clock in the morning , cleaning up vomit , dealing with customers ... We could ask ' how do we get @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ then again , you do n't want grumpy waiters . " <p> But there are way more people making beer than ever before . Incredibly , New Zealand now has 130 breweries . There 's been a 63% increase in home brew equipment listed on TradeMe in the last 12 months , while in the past two years , New Zealand-made craft beer has experienced its first big boost in sales , expanding by more than 40% . <p> And while the traditional middle New Zealand brands are in decline , premium beers are growing strongly -- up $35m in five years -- which suggests that everyone is getting a little more fancy-feast in their beer choices . We 're becoming a nation of beeries ! <p> Nowhere is this more apparent than the new Brothers Brewery in Mt Eden . A brewery and bar with a narrow and basic but delicious menu , the place is rammed with young families on weekends . It caters for both the emerging craft beer nerd market , wanting to sample experimental limited runs , while also selling many jugs of more sessionable lagers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ thing with craft is it 's a continuum -- all those deeply serious conversations about flavour profiles and hoppiness , and all the cool new specialist craft beer bars opening tend to mask the fact that craft beer is still a small segment of the total market : shockingly , all of New Zealand 's craft beer sales put together is less than the sales of DB 's Export Citrus alone . <p> But even big breweries like DB , who commissioned this story , are excited about it . It 's gotten people talking about beer , it 's improving customer knowledge . Most importantly , it 's driving the average punter out of a rut . <p> " When I was a kid , you 'd walk into a pub and there 'd be six beers on tap , " says DB 's Auckland beer ambassador Steve Fabrello . " I 'm currently putting in a bar in Hamilton that will have 18 brands on tap . The consumer 's palate and interest has grown so much that they want to experiment . Generally , they go back to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when , for such a long time , people would buy what they knew . " <p> John Frith , DB 's South Island ambassador says this can be a blessing and a curse for small brewers . " One thing craft breweries struggle to do is get past the third batch , because the craft beer drinker is not as loyal , because they want to try different things . The volume will stutter as the craft drinker waits for the newest release . " Other tiers of craft beer fan are closer to mainstream beer fans in their behaviour , resulting in craft breweries adding more-sessionable IPAs and lagers to their line-up to maintain their initial growth . It 's a trend seen in New Zealand 's flagship new generation craft breweries , with Tuatara , Liberty and Garage Project releasing more accessible beers in price-pointed six packs over the past year . <p> In this respect , they 're starting to meet bigger breweries in the middle , as the likes of Monteith 's release higher alcohol and hop-heavy APAs in 500ml bottles , along with a greater @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ breweries , from the cultiest craft to the traditional giants , are bringing new lower-alcohol beers to the market which try to not sacrifice flavour on the way . <p> For hardcore craft beer drinkers , the most exciting trend on the horizon is sour beers , lambics and salt and coriander-inflected gose ( pronounced more or less like the demon goddess Gozer at the end of Ghostbusters ) , which is even more challenging on the palate . ( Clearly this works : about a third of DB Export Citrus drinkers are not usually beer drinkers . ) <p> Americans are also drinking five times as much cider as they did five years ago , New Zealand 's consumption is up too : amongst millennials , around 40% of women and 20% of guys are now drinking cider . <p> I was oddly relieved to hear that in back-country pubs , they 're still loyal to their local beer , and when they walk into a pub , the only decision they have to make is bottle or jug -- with maths as the most critical driver . <p> " You enter Tui country and go into a pub which has a dozen beers on tap , and people are sitting around a table with a Tui quart bottle because @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ volume coming out of the tap , " Biedermann says . <p> In Tui country , they may be less eager to try a gose , which I 've seen described as tasting " like it had been squeegeed off the back of a German day labourer toiling in a coriander processing plant " .
